Katalog Berita (Halaman 28/57)

#5824. Justice Alito warns America must preserve 3 core principles regardless of country’s ‘atmosphere’

Supreme Court Justice Samuel Alito discusses retirement rumors on 'Fox News Sunday.'

Supreme Court Justice Samuel Alito recently laid out three principles Americans must cling to to ensure the integrity of the republic: freedom of speech, religious liberty and racial equality.

"I'm not saying we're entering the dark age, but these are things that we have to preserve no matter what the atmosphere is in the country or the world at the time," Alito said during a fireside chat with Mollie Hemingway, editor-in-chief at the Federalist, which followed the Antonin Scalia Memorial Dinner at the 2026 National Lawyer's Convention in Washington D.C.

The remarks come on the heels of the blue tsunami of the 2026 midterms, with Republicans losing 42 House seats and eight Senate seats, with the Alaska U.S. Senate race still uncalled. Democrats are set to take control of Congress with eight lawmakers backed by the Democratic Socialists of America. Both incoming and returning members have pressed for investigations and subpoenas targeting President Donald Trump and his administration.

United States Supreme Court Associate Justice Samuel Alito poses for an official portrait at the East Conference Room of the Supreme Court building on October 7, 2022 in Washington, DC. (Alex Wong/Getty Images)

DEMOCRATS SEIZE HOUSE CONTROL IN STUNNING BLUE TSUNAMI, VOW RETRIBUTION AGAINST TRUMP ADMIN

Alito's remarks also come amid the ongoing Iran war, which has destabilized oil supply chains across the globe, causing the price of goods and gas to jump.

The 76-year-old jurist said preserving freedom of speech is paramount, warning that "you cannot have Democratic self-fulfillment if you cannot have robust free speech."

"Even speech that is hurtful to some of the people who hear what is being said," Alito said.

He also stressed the importance of freedom of religion, even as the Supreme Court is set to rule on at least two major cases this term centered on religious freedom: St. Mary Catholic Parish v. Roy and Grand v. City of University Heights.

President Donald J. Trump greets Associate Justice of the Supreme Court Samuel Alito as he departs from a ceremony to swear in Secretary of Defense Mark Esper in the Oval Office at the White House on Tuesday, July 23, 2019, in Washington. (Jabin Botsford/The Washington Post via Getty Images)

SUPREME COURT OFFERS SUPPORT FOR CATHOLIC PRESCHOOLS CHALLENGING STATE FUNDING REQUIREMENTS

"I think that we have placed religious liberty on a sounder footing than it was on 20, 25 years ago," Alito said.

"Religious liberty is central to human dignity, and in a society that is as religiously diverse as this country, we've got people who adhere to practically every religion that exists in the world," Alito continued. "We are not going to be able to get along, I think, amicably, productively, if we don't respect everybody's right to follow whatever religion they want, or for people of faith, they're also free to do that."

He rounded off his trifecta by saying that America must maintain racial equality, declaring it "essential for our pluralistic society."

Supreme Court Associate Justices Samuel Alito and Clarence Thomas wait to leave the stage after the inauguration ceremonies in the Rotunda of the U.S. Capitol in Washington, D.C., on Jan. 20, 2025. (Chip Somodevilla/AFP via Getty Images)

FEDERAL JUDGE TOSSES TRUMP ADMIN HARVARD ADMISSIONS LAWSUIT, LEAVES DOOR OPEN TO FUTURE CHALLENGE

"That is the principle that Dr. Martin Luther King famously articulated in his speech at the Lincoln Memorial, where he said he looked forward to the day when children would be judged by the content of their character and not by the color of their skin," Alito said.

"If we don't hold to that principle, then I think the society is going to start to come apart," Alito said.

The Supreme Court agreed in October to hear Association for Education Fairness v. Montgomery County Board of Education this term, a challenge brought by parents of Asian American students alleging that a Maryland school district’s admissions policies unlawfully discriminate despite appearing race-neutral.

Alito mentioned the Supreme Court's 2023 ruling in Students for Fair Admissions v. Harvard, which ended affirmative action in college admissions, as among the most consequential rulings from the Supreme Court in the past few decades.

TRUMP CHEERS ALITO STAYING FOR ANOTHER SUPREME COURT TERM: 'ONE OF THE GREATEST OF ALL TIME'

Alito's discussion comes as questions swirl about his possible plans to retire. In a Bloomberg article published after the midterms, Alito said that the Democrats' sweep did not change his plans to stay on the Supreme Court.

Alito, nominated by President George W. Bush, has served on the bench since 2006.

"With those, with those 20 years, I jumped to 42nd in the the list of all-time service by Supreme Court justices," Alito said.

Elaine Mallon is a writer for Fox News Digital and Fox Business covering national politics.

#5822. The EU’s First AI Summit

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

World Brief: The EU’s First AI Summit

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at the European Union’s artificial intelligence ambitions, dual car bombings in Russia, and an alleged Iranian strike on a U.S. radar system.

European Union leaders convened in Dublin on Friday for the bloc’s first-ever summit on artificial intelligence. With the continent expected to fall short of its chip-manufacturing goals, the conference may be a pivotal opportunity for the EU to develop a unified policy that aims to catch up with the world’s AI powerhouses: the United States and China.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at the European Union’s artificial intelligence ambitions, dual car bombings in Russia, and an alleged Iranian strike on a U.S. radar system.

European Union leaders convened in Dublin on Friday for the bloc’s first-ever summit on artificial intelligence. With the continent expected to fall short of its chip-manufacturing goals, the conference may be a pivotal opportunity for the EU to develop a unified policy that aims to catch up with the world’s AI powerhouses: the United States and China.

The 2023 European Chips Act sought to double the continent’s semiconductor production global market share from roughly 10 percent to 20 percent by 2030 and attract billions of dollars in foreign tech investment. This legislation was a way for Europeans to say, “we want to be serious about this, and the only way to be competitive is to engage in the same practices, because otherwise you’re just never going to get manufacturing back to your shores,” Bruce Andrews, then-Intel’s corporate vice president, told FP’s Rishi Iyengar in 2024.

Since then, though, the EU has emerged as a more passive actor in AI’s advancement. A March report by Eurostat, the bloc’s statistics office, found that 1 in 5 EU enterprises used at least one AI technology in 2025, compared to less than 1 in 10 businesses a few years ago. Yet, in installed data center capacity alone, the bloc, with its 11.92 gigawatts, remains double-digits behind the United States (53.73 gigawatts) and China (31.9 gigawatts).

Such stagnation has EU leaders concerned that the continent is being left behind. “AI is the most important technology of our time,” European Council ‌President António Costa wrote on X on Friday. “Europe must make the most of its benefits, from higher productivity to better public services. We must also manage the risks and changes it will bring—and help set global standards for responsible governance and safe, human-centric AI.”

Yet the summit’s AI agenda may be derailed by other issues—particularly, who will represent European interests in future Russia-Ukraine peace talks.

On Thursday, Russian President Vladimir Putin told U.S. special envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner that he would allow European officials to join the next round of negotiations. Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky, who is also in Dublin this week for talks with EU leadership, applauded Putin’s concession. However, who will represent the continent’s delegation remains unclear.

“If Europe could identify one person to speak on their behalf, that would be a bigger achievement than getting the Russians to include them in the negotiation,” Kushner said on Thursday. Among the names floated as possible options are Costa, EU foreign-policy chief Kaja Kallas, Polish Prime Minister Donald Tusk, and Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni.

The resignation of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is also likely to steal the summit’s attention. Huang stepped down on Thursday over his alleged extramarital affairs, including one that he admitted to with a suspected Chinese spy. Nvidia is the world’s leading producer of the advanced semiconductor chips used to power AI and data centers.

Assassination attempts. Russian authorities are investigating a series of assassination attempts this week, after two separate car bombings in as many days targeted senior pro-Kremlin officials. It is unclear who was responsible for the incidents; however, Ukraine’s intelligence agency has previously used similar operations to attack dozens of Russian officers and operatives. Kyiv has claimed responsibility for a few of these operations, including the killing of Lt. Gen. Igor Kirillov in December 2024, but it has not commented on this week’s bombings.

On Thursday, local media reported an explosion in St. Petersburg’s Pushkinsky District, an area known for housing Russian military personnel. The attack killed a senior Russian military official believed to be a colonel in the country’s air force. The following day, a car bomb in Moscow severely wounded Sergei Zavorotniy, a former senior aide to exiled former Ukrainian Prime Minister Mykola Azarov, who fled Kyiv in 2014 after Ukraine’s Maidan revolution toppled the country’s pro-Russian president.

Both attacks occurred following a new round of U.S.-mediated Russia-Ukraine peace talks in Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates. Those negotiations made little headway, apart from an agreement to exchange prisoners. However, Putin later expressed his willingness to hold a future round of dialogue with European representatives present.

Alleged U.S.-Iran strikes. Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) claimed on Friday to have destroyed a U.S. early-warning radar system in Bahrain in what would be Tehran’s first direct attack on a U.S. military installation in months. The IRGC said the operation was in retaliation for a recent U.S. airstrike on an Iranian communications tower overlooking the Strait of Hormuz. U.S. Central Command, however, denied that its radar system was damaged, instead claiming to have intercepted two Iranian drones that were heading toward Bahrain.

The alleged radar system strike came the same day that a tanker carrying liquified natural gas (LNG) reportedly hit a naval mine in the strait. The IRGC navy told state media that the ship was using an “unauthorized and dangerous route” when it came into contact with the underwater explosive and that it had ignored repeated warnings to avoid the area.

As traffic through the critical passageway has started to return, the number of maritime incidents has also climbed. October was the busiest month for LNG exports from the Persian Gulf region since war first broke out in late February. According to the Washington Institute for Near East Policy, though, last month also saw the highest number of attacks on ships in the strait.

Vance’s new mandate. U.S. President Donald Trump directed Vice President J.D. Vance to move off the Iran portfolio on Thursday and instead focus his “complete and total attention” on alleged corruption in Democratic Party-run states and cities. The shift comes after Vance blamed the administration’s handling of the Iran war for Democrats’ sweeping wins in recent midterm elections.

Vance has long had a heavy hand in U.S.-Iran negotiations. Despite being a skeptic of the war, the vice president has helped shape the conflict’s objectives, led the charge for a precarious memorandum of understanding, and engaged with top Iranian hard-liners to try to revive peace talks. Moving him off the Iran agenda therefore marks a significant shift in how Trump appears to want to handle the conflict.

“We don’t need a deal with Iran,” Trump wrote on Truth Social on Thursday. “Those days have long since passed. We’ve got their money, Hundreds of Billions of Dollars, and we’ve got their Strait!”

The change-up also signals the White House’s concerns about a looming Democrat-run Congress. Incoming Democratic lawmakers have vowed to investigate Trump’s business dealings and other potential avenues of corruption when they take power in January. In response on Thursday, Trump accused Democratic Party leadership—particularly in the states of California, Illinois, and Minnesota—of stealing billions of dollars every month, without citing evidence.

On Sunday, South African authorities announced the results from Nov. 4 local elections. Why was the election a historic one for the African National Congress party?

A. The party won a majority in every province except one. B. It marked the party’s worst-ever results. C. The party won a majority in the Western Cape province for the first time. D. Two-thirds of the party’s election winners were women.

Finland already has a reputation for being the happiest country in the world. Now, Helsinki is hoping to share that wealth with 16 lucky singles from around the world. In February, Finland will host a four-day, food-oriented group date alongside local Finnish residents also looking for love. Participants will taste some of the country’s most iconic dishes, from sautéed reindeer to bread cheese; relax in traditional saunas; and explore the Nordic nation’s cultural landmarks.

FP’s World Brief writer can personally attest that the Finnish sauna experience alone provides an almost unparalleled level of relaxation. Just follow the golden rule: What is said in the sauna stays in the sauna.

B. It marked the party’s worst-ever results.

No party won an outright majority, a further sign that South Africa’s political landscape is fragmenting, FP’s Nosmot Gbadamosi reports in Africa Brief.

#5820. The World’s Last Chance for Peace

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

PEDRO SÁNCHEZ is President of the Government of Spain.

At some point during the last decade, the world started accepting war as inevitable. Wars of aggression, from Ukraine to Lebanon to Iran, are on the rise, bringing horror, ruin, and the specter of a wider international confrontation. Governments increasingly treat war as something to budget and prepare for. Military spending is climbing, technological and arms races are intensifying, and more and more countries are considering whether they should acquire a nuclear deterrent to guarantee their security.

Nothing is more dangerous than the normalization of war. It represents a return to a norm that persisted for most of human history, when war was the main organizing principle of the international system. Armed conflict was the natural way for states to settle disputes or enlarge their territory at the expense of weaker neighbors.

Yet the vast wreckage of World Wars I and II in the first half of the twentieth century made the status quo unacceptable. Countries united behind an effort to turn war from a legitimate instrument of statecraft into an outlawed act. Wars did not end in 1945, but for years after they were overwhelmingly condemned by the international community.

In recent decades, however, governments’ commitment to the cause of peace has weakened. Millions of people took to the streets in hundreds of cities to oppose the U.S.-led invasion of Iraq in 2003, but the war proceeded nonetheless. Fought on false premises and in violation of international law, it brought destruction to Iraq, fed regional instability, and fueled resentment and terrorism directed at Western countries. The conflict also marked a turning point. Ever since, the pursuit of peace has steadily receded from the international agenda amid deepening geopolitical competition and inattention or indifference to the multilateral efforts that for decades discouraged acts of war.

At the end of this path lies another world war—the catastrophe the twentieth-century order was designed to prevent. With that dire future looming, governments must relearn the art of opposing war. It is the responsibility of every country, including my own, Spain, to take direct action that constrains others’ ability to wage war and to strengthen the international institutions that will serve as guarantors of peace.

The success of this project will depend on an expansive multilateralism that I call “maxilateralism”—cooperation that embraces as many countries as possible, rather than being limited to small, ad hoc groupings. Its ambition is to make war increasingly impractical, even unimaginable. In 2003, protesters across Spain chanted “No a la guerra!’’—“No to war!” The world cannot afford to ignore them again.

International efforts to reject war as an instrument of national policy date back to the Kellogg–Briand Pact of 1928, a treaty signed in Paris by 15 countries and joined by dozens more later. It was insufficient to prevent World War II, but it planted a seed. When representatives of 50 countries gathered in San Francisco in 1945 to create the United Nations, they revived the spirit and principles of Kellogg–Briand and gave them what they had lacked: institutional form. The UN Charter prohibited the threat or use of force against the territorial integrity or political independence of another state. And, unlike its 1928 predecessor, it backed up the prohibition with institutions empowered to enforce it.

In the decades that followed, these institutions established an order that penalized violations and created incentives for diplomacy and cooperation. At the same time, public opposition to war was growing stronger around the world. Popular defiance of Cold War militarism was visible in worldwide resistance to the Vietnam War and in mass movements against nuclear weapons. Pacifism reached its high-water mark in the 1990s, when a vibrant global civil society mobilized to defend humanitarian causes and when the United Nations held sufficient authority to champion a sweeping peace agenda.

Today, however, there is more armed conflict than at any time since 1946. According to the Uppsala Conflict Data Program, there were 65 active conflicts involving governments in 2025, double the number in 2010. An estimated 250,000 people died in those conflicts—the highest annual death toll in 30 years, since the Rwandan genocide. Close to 120 million people worldwide have been forcibly displaced by war.

Worryingly, wars of aggression—which violate the UN Charter—are back. Russia invaded Ukraine. The United States and Israel attacked Iran. And as fighting continues year after year in places such as Lebanon, Sudan, and Yemen, it is becoming clear that war is being accepted as a normal occurrence.

There is no sign of the trend slowing down. Military budgets have surged to levels not seen in generations, at the expense of social and development spending. In the past decade alone, global defense spending has increased by more than 40 percent, with many countries doubling their military budgets. The nearly $3 trillion the world spends annually on defense is close to the GDP of the entire African continent. Even countries with a long tradition of military restraint, such as Denmark, Germany, Japan, and Norway, are rearming rapidly. Some are reviving military service programs.

A renewed threat of nuclear war is cause for still greater dread. All nine nuclear-armed states are modernizing their arsenals, boosting their destructive capabilities. China’s nuclear arsenal is estimated to have increased from 200 to 300 warheads in the late 2010s to 620 this year. North Korea has also expanded its arsenal from an estimated ten to 20 warheads in 2018 to around 60 this year, and it has developed increasingly sophisticated launch platforms. In March, France announced the first increase in its nuclear warhead stockpile since 1992. The United States and Russia, the world’s two largest nuclear powers, have failed to preserve the last remaining treaty constraining their strategic arsenals. And a growing number of nonnuclear powers have started to consider acquiring arsenals of their own.

Simultaneously, artificial intelligence is powering ever more dangerous weapons of war. Human oversight is being progressively reduced as AI makes it possible to automate cyberattacks against power grids, hospitals, and financial systems. AI systems have hacked government bodies and enabled the mass production of deepfakes and disinformation campaigns.

The integration of AI into nuclear technologies poses an existential threat: as AI speeds up decision-making, it reduces the time available for human judgment. Similarly, the use of AI in biotechnology could dramatically lower barriers to harmful experimentation and the development of bioweapons. In both areas, this technology is elevating the risk of miscalculation, unintended escalation, and mass destruction to a level higher than ever before.

Together, these dynamics are multiplying the chances that any crisis will quickly spiral into planetary annihilation. Countries are building the instruments of catastrophe at the blinding speed of innovation while the instruments of control move at the much slower pace of diplomacy.

Political change is pushing the world away from peace and toward confrontation and militarization. A multipolar world is not, by definition, a more dangerous one, but the erosion of rules and institutions that manage tensions between poles has made today’s world particularly volatile. Competition among great powers is increasing, with hegemonic ambitions encouraging assertive foreign policies and revisionist states challenging established rules and borders. Belligerent nationalism is on the rise, and far-right movements are advancing a worldview in which international relations is a zero-sum game and countries must compete rather than cooperate. Even centrist leaders are embracing the language of war preparedness. Although they do not intend to start a war, their words feed a dangerous militaristic mindset.

In this context of rivalry, wars of aggression come wrapped in narratives of necessity, liberation, self-defense, or historical justice. The attackers present their operations as inevitable, having demonized their targets to justify preemptive action against them. They describe each war as an answer to insecurity—a war to end all wars.

But these wars will not make a safer world. Every time a state embarks on a war of aggression, it erodes the norms that protect all countries from the arbitrary use of force—including the aggressor itself.

Wars exact immense costs. They shatter societies and interrupt lives. In 2025, at least one civilian died in an armed conflict every 14 minutes, according to UN records, and more than five million people were forced to flee their countries. The economic costs are high, too; the U.S. and Israeli war with Iran has severely disrupted maritime trade and air traffic, driving up the prices of energy, fertilizer, and other commodities, and millions of people across the world are suffering the consequences.

War also creates new dangers. Once the fighting begins, its course is unpredictable. Conflict tends to draw in new actors and spill into neighboring countries. The war in Iran serves, again, as a prime example. Since February 2026, Iran has struck targets in several Gulf countries and other neighbors—actions Spain has consistently condemned. Attacks on civilian and commercial vessels threaten broader regional stability. And the war has reignited conflicts in Iraq, Lebanon, and Yemen.

Finally, wars rarely produce lasting peace. Instead, they deepen grievances and entrench divisions that fuel future violence. Russia’s war in Ukraine, for instance, has destroyed trust in Moscow. The genocide in Gaza has inflamed anger toward Israel, potentially leading to new waves of terrorism. And the war against Iran has further destabilized the Middle East, hindering cooperation across the region.

The world has a responsibility to resist the normalization of wars. Remaining silent is an act of cowardice and complicity. Spanish society is making its position clear: three of four Spaniards identify war as their greatest fear, and an even greater number believe another world war is on the horizon.

But it is not enough to simply condemn war in principle. The ambition of ending war must be matched with action that makes it increasingly difficult for countries to start, sustain, and justify armed conflict.

Spain has endeavored to take such action. It has defended the international laws that stand between an order governed by rules and one governed by force. Spain has consistently denounced armed attacks that violate the UN Charter.

From day one of the war in Gaza, Spain has been calling for an immediate and permanent cease-fire, the release of all hostages, and the unrestricted delivery of humanitarian assistance. In May 2024, Spain recognized the state of Palestine in the hope that this step would help advance a negotiated and lasting solution to the conflict. And Spain has actively supported the efforts of the International Court of Justice and the International Criminal Court to hold Israeli leaders accountable for violations of international humanitarian law. Israel had the right to self-defense after the October 7 terrorist attacks, but that right does not justify genocide in Gaza or the expansion of Israel’s illegal occupation of the West Bank.

Deterring war also requires raising the costs of aggression. The rest of the world must ensure that those who choose to wage war cannot do so without paying a price. Throughout Russia’s war in Ukraine, for example, the European Union has introduced a series of sanctions on Russia, which Spain has consistently supported. Spain has also provided financial, humanitarian, and military assistance to Ukraine, helping it defend its territory and deny Russia the spoils of war.

Countries must refuse to help belligerents expand existing conflicts. Spain, for instance, has maintained an arms embargo on Israel since 2023. This year, it also denied the United States the use of its territory and facilities for the war against Iran. Spain’s decision was not an act of hostility toward an ally. Nor was it an expression of neutrality or sympathy for the Iranian regime. It was, rather, a judgment that getting involved would enable escalation of the conflict, deepening regional and global insecurity. To keep its commitment to peace, Spain had to be willing to say no, even when doing so carried a political cost. Had more countries done the same, the wars in Gaza and Iran would have been far harder and costlier to wage.

Investing in prosperity is also essential. Security is strengthened when societies are resilient, inequalities are reduced, and citizens can rely on public institutions. Spain’s decision last year to opt out of NATO’s proposed defense spending target of five percent of GDP reflected this understanding. This position did not stem from a lack of commitment to European defense—in fact, my government has championed Europe’s push for strategic autonomy and has tripled net defense spending over the eight years I have been in office. But military deterrence and social welfare must reinforce each other. By keeping defense spending proportionate, Spain has preserved the fiscal space to invest an additional 145 billion euros in social protection, housing, health care, and education, as well as more than 30 billion euros in the green transition—programs that make society more cohesive and adaptable, and therefore more secure.

Yet one country’s efforts can accomplish only so much. Starving war of its lifelines requires all hands on deck, a collective effort rooted in genuine multilateralism. This is the only strategy that has worked before. The multilateral system built after World War II spanned the globe, and it helped keep peace among great powers and prevent a third world war.

Reinforcing it will not be an easy task. Geopolitical competition and widespread mistrust have weakened the United Nations, international financial bodies, regional organizations, and other multilateral institutions. Many countries perceive them as ineffective and trapped in perpetual crisis, so they have turned to alternative frameworks to try to end wars.

Some have tried unilateral approaches, pursuing transactional bargains that end up benefiting economic and political elites while sidelining the populations affected by the conflict. The cease-fires or peace deals they achieve may be preferable to continued war, but any peace imposed through power politics rather than law rests on shaky foundations.

Other countries, frustrated with multilateralism, have tried recruiting coalitions of the willing, or other “minilateral” groups, to provide security guarantees or reach ad hoc arrangements on matters of trade and technology. This approach might be a temporary solution to the paralysis of traditional institutions, but its exclusivity further fragments an already divided international order. What is needed is not minilateralism but maxilateralism. International cooperation is powerful when it is, or aspires to be, universal. Leaving part of the world outside of multilateral efforts, moreover, is a recipe for confrontation down the road.

The twentieth-century system was not perfect. The application of its rules has been uneven, its standards inconsistently upheld, its procedures frustratingly slow. But it is undeniable that the world would be much more dangerous without it. To take just one example, it was a lattice of international treaties—including the Nuclear Nonproliferation Treaty, the Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces Treaty, and the Limited Test Ban Treaty—that ended a wildly expensive arms race that was driving the world to the brink of nuclear war.

All of this institutional architecture is now eroding. Yet without it, the world cannot aspire to preserve peace.

Strengthening multilateral institutions must be at the center of a global campaign to relegate war to history. This will require substantial reform to restore these institutions’ legitimacy and their capacity to act, prioritizing measures that will contribute the most to peace and security.

The first is increasing representation at the United Nations and the Bretton Woods institutions. An update to the structure of the UN Security Council is particularly important, given that body’s primary responsibility for the maintenance of international peace. A Security Council that gives permanent seats and a right to veto only to the five victors of World War II, which together account for barely a quarter of the global population, does not reflect today’s world. The council’s elected membership should be expanded and the terms of those members should be extended beyond the current two years. The new seats should be given primarily to countries in underrepresented regions, particularly Africa and Latin America. Regional organizations such as the European Union and the African Union should also be represented in the council, potentially taking over seats currently occupied by states. A more representative Security Council would not merely be fairer; it would also be more effective. Countries are more likely to defend and enforce international rules when they consider themselves to be authors of those rules.

Permanent members’ veto power must also be strictly limited, particularly when the Security Council considers cases of mass atrocities, genocide, crimes against humanity, and war crimes. Ultimately, the veto should be abolished outright. Only then can the council respond to all acts of aggression without being paralyzed by the objections of a single state.

A similar spirit should guide structural change at international financial institutions, particularly the International Monetary Fund and the World Bank. The distribution of voting power in both still reflects twentieth-century economic balances, and by convention, a European always leads the IMF and an American leads the World Bank. This must end. Emerging economies need better representation, and leadership positions should be open to all candidates regardless of nationality. Responsibilities within institutions such as the World Trade Organization should also evolve as economic growth moves countries that were once categorized as “developing” into the ranks of the wealthy. Fairer economic governance would ease the inequality and resentment that so often feed instability and conflict.

Another key reform will involve reinvigorating the international framework that limits the risk of nuclear escalation. Some of that work depends on the nuclear powers; it is up to the United States and Russia, for example, to negotiate a successor to the bilateral New START treaty that preserves verifiable limits on strategic nuclear deployments. And this time, China should be part of the discussions.

Yet the broader effort requires the engagement of all countries. Their priorities should include ratifying the Comprehensive Nuclear Test Ban Treaty to prevent a resumption of nuclear testing and advancing negotiations on a Fissile Material Cutoff Treaty, which would ban production of highly enriched uranium and plutonium for nuclear weapons. All countries must also work together to regulate the use of AI in the military domain, especially when it comes to nuclear weapons, to preserve human control over launch decisions and reduce the risk of catastrophic errors. Nonnuclear powers should commit to not acquiring nuclear weapons themselves, and they should use every tool at their disposal to encourage nuclear powers to reduce their arsenals, strengthen verification mechanisms, and rebuild the diplomatic guardrails that for decades have helped prevent nuclear catastrophe.

Technology can help with some of this. Advances in satellite imagery, remote sensors, and data analytics can increase transparency and make compliance with arms control agreements more observable. But more must be done to avoid situations that can escalate into war. If nuclear-armed states agree to lower operational readiness and strengthen crisis communication, that can help ensure that governments have the time and the means to defuse dangerous situations. Each country must protect its command-and-control systems from cyberattacks and AI-driven manipulation of information, so that a false alarm or miscalculation never leads to nuclear war.

The final priority is to ensure that the international justice system can enforce courts’ decisions. Dismantling the International Criminal Court, as some countries are trying to do, would only ensure that the world’s gravest crimes go unpunished. The ICC should not only prosecute cases of genocide, aggression, war crimes, and crimes against humanity but also have the authority to bring to justice those who are responsible for these crimes. When a state becomes a party to the Rome Statute, the treaty that created the ICC, it agrees to submit itself to the court’s jurisdiction. Yet if that state refuses to cooperate or surrender a suspect, there is no real punishment, only an embarrassing censure. This must change. States that shelter individuals indicted by the ICC should face legal, diplomatic, and financial consequences. Starting and waging war should carry a real cost.

Proposals to reform international institutions have long struggled to gain traction, but the coming election of a new UN secretary-general offers a rare opportunity to inject fresh momentum into these efforts. Were the UN to appoint its first female leader, it would be a powerful symbol of renewal, inclusion, and institutional adaptation. The organization cannot truly claim to speak on behalf of all humanity until it has had a woman leading it. Reaching this milestone could kindle a new sense of ambition and instill greater confidence in the capacity of international institutions to reform themselves—and to reclaim their role in constraining war.

Halting the world’s march toward war will require the broadest possible commitment. All countries, large and small, must refuse to look away or to participate in escalatory action. They must stand by the international norms that constrain war today, and reform and empower the institutions that will make it impossible tomorrow.

Ultimately, what will make war unimaginable is a positive peace agenda that addresses the root causes of insecurity. Every step toward eradicating poverty, expanding access to quality education, advancing gender equality, strengthening democratic institutions, and increasing disaster resilience also eases the conditions that produce violence.

International financial institutions must be able to deliver financing quickly, at scale, and at reasonable cost, so that no developing country is forced to choose between servicing its debt and investing in its people. Multilateral development banks must be able to mobilize funds for the green and digital transitions, so that climate migration and competition over resources do not become drivers of interstate conflict. And the World Health Organization’s Pandemic Agreement must be finalized and put into force to ensure that all countries will be able to access vaccines and treatments when the next global health emergency strikes.

This is why Spain has sought to place development at the heart of its foreign policy. The Spanish government has championed the UN’s Sustainable Development Goals for 2030, and last year the city of Seville hosted the Fourth International Conference on Financing for Development, where delegates committed to easing the debt burden of vulnerable countries and unlocking investment for sustainable development. Spain has also promoted initiatives such as the Global Hub on Debt for Development Swaps, launched with the World Bank; the multilateral Debt Suspension Clause Alliance, which allows vulnerable countries to pause debt payments after disasters; and its own mechanism to provide up to 60 million euros in debt relief for developing countries each year. In addition, Spain has backed climate finance, global health, gender equality, food security, and social protection initiatives in partnership with multilateral institutions and international organizations. These development policies are peacebuilding policies, too.

A larger project to rally the world behind legal norms and genuine multilateralism may seem unrealistic. But we have seen what happens when the normalization of war drags humanity into hell. The men and women who emerged from the ruins of World War II, the darkest chapter in human history, found a way to construct a multilateral world order. The question before our generation is whether we will preserve the rule of law or allow a return to rule by force. Whether we build the world of tomorrow through foresight and cooperation, or through the chaos and pain of another world war. We must choose wisely while the choice is still ours to make.

A Peace Deal in Ukraine Could Further Normalize What Was Once Taboo

How the Continent Can Survive American Antagonism and Come Out Stronger

Stephen E. Biegun and Emma Chanlett-Avery

#5803. Democrat Jonathan Kreiss-Tomkins wins race for Alaska governor, replaces a Republican

Jonathan Kreiss-Tomkins, a Democrat, has won the race for governor of Alaska and will replace the outgoing Republican. Here he's seen at a polling station at the University of Alaska Anchorage on Election Day. Eric Stone/Alaska Public Media hide caption

Democrat Jonathan Kreiss-Tomkins, a former state representative from the small island community of Sitka, won the race for Alaska governor, according to a race call by the Associated Press. At 37, he'll be the youngest governor in state history.

Kreiss-Tomkins will replace outgoing Gov. Mike Dunleavy, a term-limited Republican who has been governor since 2018. The state's last Democratic governor was Tony Knowles, who left office in 2002.

Kreiss-Tomkins, known by his initials JKT, made his "high-energy, bipartisan, problem-solving approach" a centerpiece of his campaign.

He's said he plans to build an administration of Democrats, Republicans and independents and described himself at a debate as a "man of the middle." He was endorsed by three former governors — a Democrat, a Republican and an independent — along with Republican Sen. Lisa Murkowski.

Alaskans have had enough of zero-sum partisan politics, he said in an interview just before voting at a precinct in Anchorage on Election Day.

"I strongly believe people just want things to work, and want people to work together," Kreiss-Tomkins said.

The AP race call came after over a week of counting votes, including those coming in by mail and from remote parts of the state. In the ranked-choice system the state uses, he faced three Republican candidates, including one backed by President Trump. Winning requires getting a majority of the votes.

Voters were also facing a question on the ballot about whether to scrap that unique, ranked-choice system. That proposal failed, according to another AP race call, keeping the system in place.

With Kreiss-Tomkins' win and others in the midterms, Democrats will hold 30 governors' offices across the country, up from 24 now.

Kreiss-Tomkins will take office in a state where voters have loudly complained that things aren't working as they should.

Rising costs and relatively flat state funding have forced schools to close and class sizes to rise. Already-high costs for food, fuel and housing have risen sharply, and a looming shortage of easily accessible natural gas near the state's population centers is threatening to push prices higher. And Alaskans have been migrating out of the state for more than a decade.

The Republicans in the race were Trump-backed business owner Bernadette Wilson, former Anchorage Mayor Dave Bronson and former Alaska Attorney General Treg Taylor.

Kreiss-Tomkins is set to take office at noon Dec. 7.

Eric Stone covers state government for Alaska Public Media.

#5800. As Democrats win big, thirst traps of male candidates take social media by storm

Sen. Jon Ossoff (D-GA) walks on stage to speak during a midterm election rally at the Cobb County Convention Center in Atlanta, on Oct. 17, 2026. Erik S. Lesser/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

If you're on social media, there's a good chance you've seen the videos: people getting hot and bothered about the young men Democrats just elected.

"I'm looking at you, Rob Sand. I'm looking at you, Jon Ossoff. I'm looking at you, El-Sayed," said fitness coach Jesse Barton, reacting to the midterm results. "I am looking, I am not the only one, and I'm very happy about what I see."

There are debates over candidates' relative levels of hotness. There are a lot of AI-generated images. And there are many, many montages of the politicians set to sexy pop music.

One of the most viral pieces of content was a parody Tiger Beat magazine cover featuring Democratic men … which Manoushka Guerrier, an LA-based chef and content-creator, took one step further.

"I created the entire Tiger Beat style personality quiz, complete with the different boy band personas," she said. The quiz gives people matches including "the Babyface" (Texas Sen.-elect James Talarico) and "the Heartthrob" (Colorado Rep.-elect Manny Rutinel).

Guerrier pays a lot of attention to politics, and she emphasizes that these guys' looks are just a bonus.

"They're young and they're ballsy and they're smart, and they're not just smart; they're brilliant," she says.

The tidal wave of attention on these young men creates a striking contrast to the first Trump midterm, in 2018. That election — after the Women's March, Me Too and the long list of assault and misconduct allegations against President Trump — yielded a record-setting wave of Democratic women. It all suggests that the thirsty social media posts aren't just a cheeky trend; they also say a lot about how Democrats have responded to the Trump era.

Two main forces are behind the wave of attention on these men according to Christina Wolbrecht, a political science professor at Notre Dame University.

"I think it's a function of our kind of society-wide debate about masculinity," she said. "And I also think it's about age."

The age side is straightforward — just ahead of the midterms, a majority of likely voters said they favored candidates with new ideas over those with experience in the job, according to a NPR/PBS News/Marist Poll.

The masculinity side, meanwhile, is in response to MAGA and its elevation of a very specific type of manhood, Wolbrecht said.

"For 10-plus years, Donald Trump has presented one very distinct version of masculinity," she said. "It's very much power over [others]. It's very much power through wealth and status."

Manliness took center stage on the Democratic side this election. Candidates leaned into it, with people like Rutinel and Michigan Senator-elect Abdul El Sayed posting weightlifting videos. El Sayed at one point did a video spoofing Love Island.

Voters noticed, posting admiring videos well before the election. Meanwhile, the media noticed and amplified this narrative, with profiles and think pieces throughout the midterm cycle, and reporting on Democrats' "rugged" and "authentic" candidates.

Amplifying young, "manly" men may have boosted the party in this election. That's because Democrats had a problem after Kamala Harris lost the presidency in 2024, according to Democratic strategist Monica Venske.

"The feeling within the straight male demographic, if you will, [was] that it just wasn't cool to be a Democrat," she said. "It wasn't sexy to be a Democrat. It's not manly to be a Democrat. That's a girly thing to be, is a Democrat."

That's why she started the Instagram account Boyfriends We Deserve, where she highlights attractive Democrats — mostly men, but a few women as well.

Venske explains she is careful in how she posts about women's looks — unlike when she talks about men, like Montana's incoming Democratic congressman.

Sam Forstag poses for a portrait at an event on a ranch outside of Drummond, Montana on October 7, 2026. Tailyr Irvine for NPR hide caption

"Like when I call Sam Forstag hot, it will most likely never, ever hurt him," she said. "But I can call someone like Keisha Lance Bottoms hot and that could hurt her every time."

She said she instead will call the women "baddies" or focus on their hair, as opposed to highlighting their bodies or overall looks.

To Venske, the Boyfriends We Deserve posts are part of making it clear that the Democratic party is a big tent that includes young men.

"I'm like, 'Look at all these manly guys who are Democrats,'" she said. "Sam Forstag is an example I usually give. He was like a smokejumper in Montana. And in his announcement video for Congress, he's fixing up a truck. If a guy who is jumping out of planes to put out fires and fixing up trucks can be a Democrat, you can be a Democrat, I promise."

All of that said, plenty of Democratic women ran and won this year, including several historic firsts: Georgia's Keisha Lance Bottoms, for example, will be the first Black woman governor. New Mexico's Deb Haaland will be the first Native American woman governor. Minnesota's Peggy Flanagan will be the nation's first Native American woman senator.

That highlights a big difference between this midterm and 2018's wave of women.

Since that election, Democrats have seen Joe Biden defeat Trump, and then watched a second woman lose the presidency to Trump. And while Democrats most certainly do still elect women — far more than Republicans — the masculinity of their male candidates received outsized attention this year.

It's possible, then, that the attention on Democratic men isn't simply lighthearted, but is part of Democrats' continual conversation about electability.

"I hear it from all sorts of people with all sorts of engagement that 2016 and 2024 proved that it took old-guy, aviator sunglasses Joe Biden to defeat Trump," Wolbrecht said, "and we need more of that and not more Kamala Harrises or Hillary Clintons."

"I think that's a mistake," she added quickly. "I think it's a misunderstanding of what happened in '16 and in '24. But the meaning we put on elections has consequences and it really shapes what parties do strategically going forward."

Democrats have struggled to win over men in recent elections. For example, Harris won 43 percent of men overall in 2024. This year, the party won 50 percent of the men's vote.

Guerrier disagrees that the attention on candidates' looks is about electability, but she thinks that for a lot of voters, this election was about changing the Democratic Party's story.

"It was just like these guys came out of nowhere, and it was just so refreshing that, wow, we can get out of this darkness and into something that is hopeful," she said.

#5796. Hegseth's failed impeachment was a warning to Democrats. Where will they go from here?

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., speaks during a news conference on Nov. 4, 2025, on Capitol Hill in Washington, D.C. Tom Brenner/Getty Images hide caption

Democrats are not in the majority yet, but the first post-election test of their ability to get things done was a failure.

The House was unable to advance an impeachment effort of Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth. Eleven Democrats were no-shows for the vote. Based on the math in this vote: If those 11 had been there and if no more Republicans showed up, the attempt would have gone forward.

Now, for those who want to see Hegseth impeached, it's fair to ask why only one Republican was in favor of it, Rep. Thomas Massie of Kentucky, who led the effort, especially after an election that saw GOP wipeouts in no small measure because of the Iran war? The GOP still controls the floor, so a Democratic speaker would have been able to leave the vote open longer for the 11 to be able to cast their votes.

But it's also fair to ask if this would have happened under Nancy Pelosi's watch? (Ironically, she was one of the 11 who didn't vote for advancing Hegseth's impeachment because she had flown back to San Francisco for a veterans' event.) Or if this kind of effort is what people want in the first place.

First, on the Pelosi point: The former House speaker and retiring congresswoman was known for the discipline she kept in her caucus. She knew where the votes were, who was voting how and why – and, most importantly, did not move forward with efforts that didn't have the votes.

Hakeem Jeffries, the heir apparent Democratic leader, is going to have the largest majority in Congress since the early 1990s. His leadership and agenda priorities are going to be scrutinized and compared to Pelosi – and this wasn't a great start to this new era.

Second, on whether impeachments are what people want to see: it depends on which people. Certainly, the Democratic base — and some beyond that — wants accountability for the Trump administration.

Voters more broadly also said corruption was a top issue in the campaign. But walking the line between reasonable accountability and oversight versus impeachments is a potential tight rope.

Democrats will have to balance the mandate they were given to bring down prices (sound familiar?) and end the war with the accountability of the Trump administration that many in the Democratic base feverishly wants and expects.

A Reuters/Ipsos poll taken after the election, found that 8-in-10 people think Democrats' focus in the first 100 days should be on the cost of living, three-quarters said lowering health care costs, 7 in 10 said lowering gas prices, while 6 in 10 said ending the Iran war and eliminating President Trump's tariffs.

What about investigations and impeachment? Only 40% said investigating the Trump administration should be a priority in those early days. But 71% of Democrats do want this to be a first 100 days focus. Only 36% of independents said the same.

Almost two-thirds said they are concerned that Democrats will spend too much time on investigations, which included, by the way, half of Democrats.

When it comes specifically to pursuing impeachment of Trump, only 33% said they think it would be good for the country. On the other hand, 63% of Democrats do. Only 30% of independents said it would be good for the country.

More than half also said they think Democrats should at least make efforts to work with the Trump administration. That, however, only included 40% of Democrats.

So this is going to be tricky to navigate for Democratic leadership, but a major part of a speaker's job is messaging, particularly when that speaker's party does not control the White House.

People are going to be watching the new Democratic majority for what it focuses on, and even with the Hegseth effort, it was clear that many conservatives were thrilled with not only how it went but that Democrats were fueling Massie's effort.

It arguably backfired and strengthened Hegseth's position within the administration. The White House and Republicans rallied around him. Even if some Republicans on Capitol Hill might have misgivings with Hegseth for one reason or another, and they and Vice President Vance think the war in Iran was the main reason for the midterm losses, opposing Democrats is far more unifying.

Politics is often (some would say too often) a team sport, and no group is more conditioned to put the T-shirt on and run the playbook than the red team.

It certainly gave Republicans more of a chance to go on offense than talking about health care, the cost of living or the war and gas prices.

Democratic leadership is certainly aware of the line it has to walk, but Jeffries is going to have to make some decisions. Asked on PBS NewsHour this week if Democrats would be impeaching more Trump officials, he said, "We will cross that bridge when we get to it."

But a House speaker is the one who builds the legislative bridges. He or she controls the agenda.

Jeffries, of course, also noted, as he has elsewhere, that affordability is a priority.

"We're committed to driving down the high cost of living in every area," he said. "That's housing, that's health care, that's groceries, that's gas, that's caregiving – to making this country affordable again for everyone."

Democrats have been given a huge opportunity with majorities in both the House and Senate — but the balance of priorities, timing, messaging and discipline are all going to be major tests.

The stakes couldn't be higher. Arguably, what Democrats focus on will lay the foundation for views of the party's competence heading into the 2028 presidential election, and they don't want to squander the advantages they have.

The Ipsos poll showed that Democrats have big leads on which party has a better plan, policy or approach for health care (42%-23%), the cost of living (40%-25%) and the economy (37%-30%).

But they have to be careful. A lot of people are not convinced that Democrats are focused on the issues that are important to them. Only 47% in the Ipsos poll said that, including only 40% of independents.

Only 44% said they're confident that Democrats are putting the best interests of the American people above their own personal interests.

So while Democrats have the wind at their backs, the jury is still out on whether people are confident in their leadership.

The five highest-performing products in each market were awarded Best in Show distinctions, while additional products were awarded Honorable Mentions based on their unique product categories.

Residential Best in Show winning companies include ConnectDER, Enerflo, Generac, Optivolt and SolarEdge. Residential Honorable Mentions were also given to products in the BOS, energy storage, inverter, panel and software categories.

#5779. Alaska voters aren't ditching ranked choice voting, top-four primaries

A voter walks into a booth at the Valley Bible Chalet to cast his ballot on Election Day, Nov. 3, in Indian, Alaska. Lindsey Wasson/AP hide caption

A ballot measure that would have repealed Alaska's nonpartisan primary system and ranked choice voting has failed, according to an overnight race call by the Associated Press.

This is the second time Alaskans voted down an effort to repeal the state's unique election system, which voters first approved in 2020. A repeal effort in 2024 also failed narrowly.

This year, though, Alaska's system was under intense nationwide scrutiny, due to the inclusion of two candidates with the same name on the four-person U.S. Senate general election ballot. That race appears likely to move on to a ranked-choice tabulation.

"Alaskans of all political stripes have spoken: They want the freedom to vote for any candidate, regardless of party, in every taxpayer-funded election," Nick Troiano — the executive director of Unite America, a philanthropic venture fund that invests in nonpartisan electoral reform — said in a statement before the AP call. "At a time when most general elections across the country are essentially coronations, the Last Frontier is showing that a better way is possible. It is time to honor the voters' decision and move forward."

Just a few states have nonpartisan primary systems that allow voters of all political affiliations to vote on the same primary ballot. While California and Washington's systems allow the top two vote getters to advance to the general election, Alaska's system allows the top four candidates to move on. Then, if no candidate gets a majority of the general election vote, ranked choice voting is used to determine the winner.

Opponents of Alaska's elections say the current system is confusing and can be manipulated.

Those opponents seized on this year's closely watched Senate race, in which an independent candidate named Dan Sullivan was alleged to be a Democratic-backed spoiler recruited to siphon votes that were meant for the Republican incumbent, also named Dan Sullivan.

Democrats denied recruiting the independent Sullivan, who made the top-four general election ballot and has defended his candidacy.

However, proponents of Alaska's system have said that the state's ranked choice voting system could blunt the effects of any confusion. Voters had the ability to rank both Dan Sullivans and not rank Democrat Mary Peltola, for example.

Massachusetts voters this year decided not to switch to a top-two nonpartisan primary system. And during the last election cycle, in 2024, several states considered measures that would create a nonpartisan system similar to Alaska's. Voters rejected all of those ballot measures.

#5773. 'We can't let them down' -- After midterm wins, Democrats turn to charting path ahead

Texas Senator-elect James Talarico speaks at the Mission Ballroom during the What's Next Denver political convention on Nov. 12, 2026. David Zalubowski/AP hide caption

DENVER — Democrats descended on Denver for a two-day conference that was half victory party, half sober look at the road ahead on delivering for the voters who elected them.

"The art of the deal to Trump is he figures out what you want, he leverages the you know what out of you, and keeps taking, taking, taking, and never delivers," Sherrill said. "My advice to Sen. Talarico is if Trump wants to work with us, we will lay out the agenda, and he can sign it."

#5768. Florida city cancels St. Patrick's Day parade, citing new anti-DEI law

Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis (shown here in October) signed the anti-DEI law that Delray Beach officials say prohibits them from putting on cultural celebrations, like a St. Patrick's Day parade. Alex Wong/Getty Images hide caption

One of the first casualties of a new Florida law that prohibits taxpayer dollars from going toward diversity initiatives is a St. Patrick's Day parade in a beach town that has been celebrating its Irish American community for nearly 60 years.

The city of Delray Beach announced this week that it was canceling its popular St. Patrick's Day festivities in compliance with a new law that takes effect on Jan. 1. The city will also withdraw its sponsorship of LGBTQ Pride celebrations in June, officials said.

The law, backed by Republican Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis is part of the state's ongoing crackdown on diversity, equity and inclusion (DEI) initiatives that the governor has called discriminatory and said have "no place" in public institutions.

The city lawyer for Delray Beach, a community in southeast Florida with picturesque beaches and a historic downtown, told the mayor that any government-sponsored celebrations of culture, ethnicity or heritage that are not a national holiday (for example: Independence Day) could run afoul of the new anti-DEI law. So, Mayor Tom Carney said, he made the difficult decision to cancel one of the city's biggest events of the year.

In past years, the parade, an annual event since 1970, wound its way through the downtown core of Delray Beach with spectators lined up several-people deep along the route. Local businesses and organizations sponsored floats and giveaways. School bands played as they marched through the streets. Participants included members of the Ancient Order of Hibernians, the largest and oldest Irish Catholic organization in the country.

"I've said it's engrained in the DNA of Delray and I do mean that. It's one of our signature events," Carney, a Republican, told NPR.

The mayor said he's hopeful that the city can find a path forward — and a way to preserve the event.

If a private sponsor were to volunteer to take over the St. Patrick's Day parade, or the June Pride celebrations, Carney said, the city would gladly hand over the reins and support the continuation of these festivities.

"These types of events we should be celebrating because in this country, people can have these events. In other countries, you can't do this. We have great freedoms here," he said. "And if the community wants something and it's not against public policy then I would support them to do it if they're using private dollars. If we can do it, we should do it."

Carney, who is of Irish descent, said the parade was privately run until about eight years ago — so returning to a private-sponsorship model would be a callback to the parade's origins. His own family, he said, was involved in keeping the parade going during the 1980s and 1990s.

Delray Beach officials said they have been inundated with emails, calls and social media messages from constituents who are angry that the community's decades-long celebration had been axed.

"It was kind of a call to action to me," Carney said. "The community really wants to have it, so we should go ahead and have it, we just need to get it privately funded."

The governor, meanwhile, has accused Delray Beach of weaponizing the new anti-DEI measure to make a political point. DeSantis said the city was "engaging in "malicious compliance" in a message he posted on X following the city's cancellation announcement.

"What they are doing is not mandated by Florida law," the governor wrote. "It's just an attempt to make a political point — a point that legacy media will no doubt try to amplify. We've seen these stunts throughout the years. Rinse and repeat."

The mayor said the city is merely trying to stay out of trouble with the law. The measure, SB1134, asserts that any official that violates the provision could face civil or legal consequences.

"The legislation has unintended consequences. The governor may genuinely believe what he believes and I take him at his word; our city attorney came up with a different conclusion," Carney said. "I'm going to leave it at that."

#5763. Judge temporarily blocks Trump from moving ahead on Washington public golf course redevelopment

Temporary fencing is seen at East Potomac Park, Saturday, Nov. 14, 2026, in Washington. Tom Brenner/AP hide caption

WASHINGTON — A federal judge on Saturday ordered a two-week delay to any work at a historic Washington public golf course not far from the White House, blocking for now President Donald Trump's push to redevelop the site along the Potomac River into a championship-level venue.

U.S. District Judge Ana Reyes issued the temporary restraining order before the East Potomac Golf Links was due to close Sunday for 15 months. Trump had announced work would begin that day despite the legal challenge initiated against the project in February by the DC Preservation League and two local golfers.

The plaintiffs argue that Trump's plan would change the character of the accessible golf course and make it unaffordable, something the Republican president denies. They had sought the stop-work order, saying that closing the course and launching work on the project would "irreparably injure plaintiffs and the public interest."

The president's public announcements on social media last month outlining the date of the closure of East Potomac Park and linking it to the renovation of the golf course weighed heavily in the decision. Trump said on Oct. 26 that "We are closing the East Potomac Golf Course Site as scheduled on November 15th in order to renovate and rebuild for Competitive (with other such projects!), Safety, Modernization, and Aesthetic reasons." He added that the goal was to renovate and rebuild "a World Championship Public Facility."

Reyes in her ruling Saturday noted that Trump's posts presented a problem for the government. "They indicate that the Washington National Plan is final and that NPS will close the Park to implement it," she wrote. "Both points favor granting Plaintiffs' motion for another temporary restraining order."

Reyes said her order "ENJOINS the government from closing East Potomac Park and the East Potomac Golf Course during the daily hours in which they were open as of November 14, 2026. It does not order the Government to undertake any active management of either. Further, for the avoidance of any doubt, the Government remains free, as it has since the onset of this litigation, to conduct repairs on any part of the Park and Course with any notice required by the existing Preliminary Injunction."

The judge said the order is in effect "through November 28, 2026, or further order from the Court, whichever comes first."

The Justice Department said in a statement Saturday that the judge acknowledged that the National Park Service can limit access to its facilities where the public is at risk of harm. "Nothing in the Court's order precludes planning for the development of a world class golf course to continue," it said. "The Department of Justice will always vociferously defend the Trump Administration's agenda in court."

Catherine Carroll, a senior legal adviser at Democracy Forward, which is representing the plaintiffs, praised the ruling. "We are grateful that once again, a court has intervened to prevent irreparable harm caused by the Trump-Vance administration's pursuit of another one of the president's vanity projects," she said. "The administration's inexplicable drive to destroy East Potomac Park would have taken away a treasured park that could never be replaced."

The park's redevelopment is one of multiple projects in the nation's capital that Trump has championed to remake the city. They include a large memorial arch, a White House ballroom and the John F. Kennedy Center for the Performing Arts, among others.

Reyes had signaled throughout a five-hour court hearing Friday that she was likely to issue some stoppage for the park closure. The only issue was how expansive it might be. Justice Department lawyer Adam Gustafson argued that the entire East Potomac Park, which includes three golf courses, a miniature course, bike riding lanes and other recreational areas, was being closed because of safety factors. He also noted that the lease between the government and the current operator, National Links Trust, was ending Sunday.

Reyes pointed out that safety had not been part of the discussion over the closure for months and that ending the lease felt more like an action that provided a premise for the closure rather than an action that led to the closure of the course about 2 miles from the White House.

Temporary fencing is seen at East Potomac Park, Saturday, Nov. 14, 2026, in Washington. Tom Brenner/AP hide caption

Even before the order, Trump lashed out in a social media post Saturday, contending that Reyes "doesn't want us to quickly and inexpensively fix up the Course which is, also, very dangerous." He said people who live in the Washington area would get a 50% discount on "any and all Rates charged" and that the redevelopment would "evolve into something very special, something which the Judge will be able to take great pride in seeing!"

Reyes' order was the latest in a series of rulings in lawsuits challenging Trump's projects that were issued just hours before construction was expected to begin. A different judge recently ordered a 14-day delay on work for the arch, a decision that came shortly after a key planning commission stacked with Trump appointees approved plans for the project.

Reyes, who was nominated to the court by Democratic President Joe Biden, had for months been reticent about approving the plaintiffs' requests for work stoppages. The plaintiffs had argued the administration was moving ahead with work related to the project despite the pending lawsuit.

Her reluctance to issue orders ended in October, spurred in part by a tree-cutting spree by the administration over a holiday weekend with little notification to the judge or plaintiffs.

Officials and government lawyers said the tree work was part of a 2019 plan that recommended the cutting. In response, the judge confronted the administration over what she portrayed as deceitful actions over its intentions for the project.

Reyes has repeatedly expressed frustration over the minute planning details she has been asked to adjudicate, including over the trees being cut down at the 106-year-old park. The Justice Department filed a request that Reyes be removed from the case because of what it called her "egregious behavior" and questioned her impartiality. Reyes has not recused herself.

The restraining order was one of two issues Reyes took up during Friday's hearing.

She announced plans last week to name Jessie Liu, a former U.S. attorney for the District of Columbia, to examine whether officials with the Interior Department and the National Park Service and attorneys with the Justice Department had violated ethics or misled her during the course of the case.

#5760. Solar and storage companies voice support for federal permitting reform

Hundreds of solar and storage companies have co-signed a letter addressed to Congress in support of the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026 (BAAJA) (S. 5653).

“You can’t lower energy costs if you can’t build the energy projects, and these companies know that this bipartisan legislation will help get America building again,” said Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA), in a press release.

BAAJA was introduced in the Senate on Sept. 30 by Sen. Shelly Moore Capito (R-WV), and co-sponsored by Sen. Mike Lee (R-UT), Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse (D-RI) and Sen. Martin Heinrich (D-NM). The legislation is focused on quickening permitting for a range of projects with federal oversight, many of them related to energy production. BAAJA specifically calls out solar photovoltaic systems, energy storage and on- and offshore wind, but also includes language for fossil fuel sources, such as natural gas, oil and coal.

If passed, the Senate bill would amend sections of the National Environmental Policy Act, Clean Water Act and Endangered Species Act of 1973 with the goal of shortening project permitting windows. According to the text, it would change and reduce which projects would need to be reviewed under those acts, such as water quality certifications; shorten the length of review periods for projects; and would not require permits for oil and gas drilling on land not owned by the federal government.

BAAJA would also grant the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission the ability to issue permits for some electricity transmission projects.

According to a SEIA analysis, if BAAJA was passed, solar energy output could increase by 167 GW between 2028 and 2035. The companies that signed this letter to Congress believe that federal permitting practices are outdated, and permitting reform is a solution to lowering energy costs.

“The message from more than 300 companies comes as voters made clear in the midterm elections that lowering energy costs is a top concern,” the group stated in a press release. “Permitting reform is urgently needed to meet rising demand and get more reliable, affordable solar and storage projects built.”

BAAJA is currently under review by the Committee on Energy and Natural Resources.
