#5671. Trump Officials Will Fund New Research on Controversial Psychedelic Drug Ibogaine

— Veterans groups, psychedelic advocates say the drug has great promise for mental heath conditions

Federal officials on Thursday announced new funding to explore the medical potential of ibogaine, a controversial psychedelic drug championed by veterans, conservative lawmakers, and podcasters aligned with President Donald Trump.

Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. said his department is awarding up to $4.9 million to prepare for the first U.S. studies of ibogaine for post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), a hard-to-treat condition linked to higher suicide and substance abuse rates among veterans.

The money will not go directly to patient research, Kennedy said, but to lay the groundwork needed to manufacture, dose, and design studies of the drug.

Ibogaine is made from a shrub native to West Africa. It can cause intense hallucinations, nausea, vomiting, tremors, and sometimes deadly irregular heart rhythms. Ibogaine is currently illegal, in the same ultra-restrictive class as LSD, ecstasy, and most other psychedelic drugs.

Veterans organizations and psychedelic advocates contend that ibogaine has great promise for intractable conditions such as PTSD, severe depression, and opioid addiction. U.S. research on the drug has essentially been frozen for decades due to ibogaine's restricted status.

Kennedy said the government will receive data and treatment guidance from Beond Inc., a Texas-based company that has treated more than 3,000 patients with ibogaine in Mexico, where the drug is not illegal.

"That is going to allow us to dramatically accelerate the clinical trial process," Kennedy said. "We're going to have, I think, a record drug approval process."

Additional funding awards may explore ibogaine's use for addiction, he said.

Veterans Affairs Secretary Doug Collins, who joined Kennedy for the announcement, said the collaborative effort would focus first on safety and "best-in-class research."

"For veterans living with PTSD, we owe them both urgency and the best science we can produce," Collins said.

Kennedy and Collins have previously voiced support for the potential of psychedelics, citing the experiences of veterans who have traveled abroad for therapy with ibogaine and other mind-altering substances.

Thursday's announcement was previewed in April, when Trump signed an executive order directing federal agencies to speed up review and research of psychedelics that could help with mental health conditions.

Three applications for psychedelics are pending review at the FDA. They include the first potential medicines made from psilocybin, the active ingredient in magic mushrooms, and MDMA, also called ecstasy. Decisions on those drugs could come within weeks or months.

Because of its safety risks, ibogaine research has moved more slowly.

The little data currently available mostly come from patients who have traveled to Mexico or other countries where ibogaine is not heavily regulated. Ibogaine clinics typically charge $10,000 to $20,000 for one course of treatment, which often involves monitoring of a patient's heart readings while emergency medical workers are on standby.

Trump's order on psychedelics came after podcaster Joe Rogan said he texted Trump about ibogaine, which he repeatedly has discussed on his show. According to Rogan, the president quickly responded: "Sounds great. Do you want FDA approval? Let's do it."

#5670. From Callback to Confidence: How Engineering an ESS for Field Reduces the Costly Callbacks

Installer on-site callbacks are the silent margin killer in residential energy management and storage. They consume labor, fuel, new project scheduling capacity, and homeowner trust in ways that rarely show up in the original bid. Across the industry, callbacks come from three sources that often overlap: system designs that are too complex to cleanly install, hardware failures after the system is in service, and challenges that arise in the field during installations. Most contractors only feel the cumulative cost years after an install have rolled past. Conversely highly reliable system hardware allows a contractor to confidently “right-size” the project price bid, helping to bring in more business in a period of industry transition.

Preventing callbacks is not only the installer’s responsibility. How the system is engineered, built, and supported all impact callbacks. FranklinWH has focused on quality products and the simplest install, with hardware held to an independently verified reliability standard. The company provides support before, during and after the installation. All of this helps to minimize callbacks. Service data shows once system complexity and hardware failure are engineered down, the dominant driver of post-install activity that remains is the installation stage itself, the real-world variability of the field. That, too, is a problem to solve at the source, not one to pass to the installer.

This paper walks through the anatomy of a callback, the hidden cost stack it creates, the actual root causes industry-wide and in our own data, and the engineering, support, and service decisions made to reduce callback risk before the truck rolls, and to absorb the cost when it can’t be avoided.

#5669. Managing Offshore Wind in the Face of Uncertainty

This report lays out key adaptive management principles and recommendations for regulators.

Despite current headwinds from the Trump administration, offshore wind energy is essential for transitioning away from harmful fossil fuels to a clean energy economy. Offshore wind could provide more than three times the electricity required to power the United States, giving the country an opportunity to fight climate change, reduce local and regional air and water pollution, and grow a new industry that supports thousands of high-quality careers.

While the need for clean energy is urgent, we can and must ensure that all offshore wind in the United States, including floating offshore wind systems, are developed responsibly and in a manner that avoids, minimizes, mitigates, and monitors for adverse impacts to biodiversity and coastal and marine ecosystems. “Adaptive management” is crucial for meeting the dual goals of facilitating a rapid transition to renewable energy while minimizing impacts to the ocean in the face of uncertainty—and continuing to do so over time.

This report explores how adaptive management can be implemented in offshore wind development, provides examples of its use in other energy sectors today, and offers clear recommendations for agencies and policymakers.

#5668. If The Temerario's Exhaust Note Stopped You Buying One, Lamborghini Has Some Good News Made From Titanium

Despite the 10,000-rpm redline and its status as a mid-engined Italian supercar, the Lamborghini Temerario makes a noise that isn't universally loved. Our own Daniel Golson says this car's twin-turbo V8 is one of the best engines he's ever experienced, and Car Throttle says it sounds "incredible." Our own Andy Kalmowitz says it sounds "broken" below 3,000 rpm, while the Throttle House/Grand Tour guys once remarked that unless you're knocking on the redline's door, the Lambo simply "doesn't sound very good."

I think the Temerario having to live in the shadow of its naturally aspirated V10 Huracán predecessor has a lot to do with this mixed reception, but either way, Lamborghini has seemingly heard the feedback and is now raising the supercar's aural ceiling by offering a titanium exhaust from Akrapovič as a factory option. The upgrade is very much positioned as a sound enhancement, not necessarily a performance one, because in the first line of its press release, Lambo says it was "developed to amplify the natural acoustic character of its V8 engine while utilizing lightweight engineering."

These new optional pipes feature a different geometry and layout, physically wider tubes, custom manifolds, and a tailored silencer architecture. Strategic insulation keeps things cool in hot zones, and NVH was carefully calibrated so specific frequencies are enhanced. Lambo says this gives the Temerario "deep notes" at low rpm and an "incisive acoustic signature" at high ones.

It's made of high-grade titanium alloys, uses precision-cast valve housings, and is hand-assembled "across every component." Lamborghini says it weighs 25.1 pounds, 12% less than the standard exhaust, which means it cuts about 3.4 pounds from the car's curb weight. The tip is black to blend in with the car's rear end, and of course it says Akrapovič on the top so everyone at Cars & Coffee can know you spent the extra dough even when the car is off. There's good news for people who already have Temerarios looking for some extra, better decibels: the part will be available as a retrofit package.

We should note that, before this, the Temerario could already be had with a titanium muffler if you went for the lightweight Alleggerita Package that also bundled in a bunch of carbon-fiber bits, but this Akrapovič system is said to be new and therefore definitely not the same thing. Akrapovič is a Slovenian company that specializes in aftermarket exhausts, and has arguably become the name in the space, a bit like how Brembo is with brakes or Recaro is with seats. Lambo previously worked with Akrapovič on upgraded exhausts for the Urus SUV and Huracán STO.

For reference, the Temerario uses a twin-turbo 4.0-liter V8 along with three electric hybrid motors to produce 907 horsepower. For those who were on the fence about buying one because it apparently doesn't sound quite as nutty as that old V10 (I mean, that's why I haven't pulled the trigger yet), perhaps these new Slovenian pipes will change things for you.

#5667. 12 Of The Longest Range Electric SUVs In 2026

Just five years ago, cracking the 300-mile mark was seen as a major achievement for electric SUVs. Today, though, it's merely the norm, with even some affordable base models, like the Equinox EV LT1 and Mustang Mach-E Select RWD, easily surpassing that figure. Heck, newcomers like the BMW iX3 50 xDrive and Lucid Gravity Grand Touring offer more than 400 miles of range with dual-motor AWD and sports-car-like acceleration.

Crucially, the electric SUVs with the longest range in 2026 prove that the compromise era is over and that they are now a viable alternative to their gas counterparts. And it's not only because of the range. Most models we listed here also top up quickly, with some electric SUVs charging at up to 400 kW. As you'd expect from EVs, they are quick off the line, too.

For this list, we ranked the SUVs based on their EPA-estimated range, save for one EPA-exempt heavyweight. Also, all listed prices include destination charges. So, let's crunch the numbers and see how this year's electric SUVs compare.

The Equinox EV is quietly making a case as the smartest electric buy for 2027. After the out-of-control price drops for 2026, it now starts at an affordable $36,795. That's slightly less than the base Hyundai Ioniq 5 ($36,900) and only a bit more than the Toyota bZ ($36,575), but the Equinox EV beats both rivals handily when it comes to range.

Even the base single-motor FWD model (220 horsepower) comes with a fairly large 85-kWh battery, which provides a class-leading EPA-estimated range of 319 miles. Meanwhile, the base Ioniq 5 only packs a 63.0-kWh battery and a 168-hp motor, enough for a 245-mile range. The Toyota bZ has an even smaller 57.7-kWh battery that provides an even shorter 236-mile range and a less potent 165-hp front motor. Granted, the Equinox EV's 150-kW peak charging rate can't match the Ioniq 5's, but it keeps up with the Toyota bZ.

Better yet, adding a second motor has a negligible effect on range, with the AWD model rated at an EPA-estimated 307 miles between charges. Choosing the 300-hp AWD model does increase performance quite significantly, though. According to Car and Driver's tests, the FWD Equinox EV needs 7.7 seconds to reach 60, while the AWD cuts that down to 5.8 seconds.

The Equinox EV isn't only good at the numbers game. Inside, it offers enough room for adults in both rows and a useful 26.4-cubic-foot trunk. It also pampers passengers with a smooth ride, and it can even be equipped with GM's Super Cruise hands-free highway driving assistance tech.

The 2027 Kia EV6 ties the Equinox EV with an identical EPA-estimated 319-mile range. However, you'll need at least the Light Long Range trim, which starts at $42,745 and packs an 84-kWh battery alongside a 225-horsepower motor in the rear. The base 167-hp model has a smaller 63.0-kWh battery and an EPA-estimated range of only 237 miles, though it starts at a lower $39,445. Since it shares the 800-volt architecture with the Ioniq 5, the EV6 can use 350-kW chargers, which top up the larger battery from 10% to 80% in less than 18 minutes.

Although it looks like a futuristic hot hatch on the outside, the EV6 is a family car first and foremost. It has a spacious interior with ample room in both seating rows and a decent 24.4-cubic-foot trunk. Also, while the Equinox EV's hood packs only electronics, the EV6 has a small frunk for charging gear or a tire repair kit.

Kia's striking-looking EV also handles better than the Equinox EV, making it the preferred choice for enthusiast drivers. Still, getting the most out of the chassis requires opting for the 320-hp dual-motor variant, which sprints to 60 in 4.5 seconds as clocked by Car and Driver. Kia shelved the 641-hp GT variant in the U.S. this year — too bad, because it offered supercar-like pace (0 to 60 in 3.4 seconds), better handling, and virtual gear shifts.

The Mustang Mach-E is a veteran of the EV space, with the first model launched for the 2021 model year. Back then, the Mach-E had an EPA-estimated range of up to 305 miles with the extended-range pack (98.8 kWh gross, 88 kWh usable). The 2027 model can travel up to 320 miles with the same usable capacity, a clear sign of the improvements Ford made over the years.

For 2027, the Blue Oval discontinued the small 73-kWh battery pack, meaning the 88.0-kWh unit is standard across models except the performance-oriented GT and Rally trims, which come with an even larger 91.0-kWh pack. As part of the 2027 lineup overhaul, Ford also launched the Mustang Mach-E Trail package with a raised suspension, Michelin CrossClimate 2 all-season tires, and a 400W onboard inverter.

Now that the Mustang Mach-E comes with the extended-range battery as standard, it also starts a bit higher than its rivals at $42,040. Still, that gets you a fairly powerful 272-horsepower motor on the rear axle, which, according to MotorTrend, sprints to 60 in 5.7 seconds. The high-performance GT and Rally models are even quicker, both reaching 60 in just 3.3 seconds.

Because of its EV architecture, the Mustang Mach-E offers generous accommodations in both rows, alongside a good 29.7-cubic-foot trunk. It's a fairly comfortable and quiet cruiser, too, with Ford now offering the BlueCruise 1.5 hands-free driving tech on the base Mustang Mach-E Select trim to make long trips more relaxing for the driver.

With a base price of $65,895 and a 325-mile EPA-estimated range (with 19-inch wheels), the Audi Q6 e-tron seems overpriced next to its non-premium alternatives. Worse yet, opting for larger 20-inch rims drops the range to 301 miles.

Still, you do get more for your money here. Most notably, every Q6 and Q6 Sportback e-tron model comes with a 456-horsepower dual-motor AWD powertrain, which Audi says propels these SUVs to 60 in 4.9 seconds. Thanks to the advanced 800-volt architecture, Audi's electric SUVs also support quick charging at up to 270 kW. That's enough to charge the big 100-kWh battery from 10 to 80% in 21 minutes on supported chargers.

Befitting a premium SUV, the Q6 e-tron is very quiet and comfortable, especially when equipped with the optional air suspension. Furthermore, the cabin is roomy enough for adults, with the 30.2-cubic-foot trunk also ready to swallow larger cargo. The coupe-like Sportback model has a smaller 26.1-cubic-foot trunk because of the sloping roof, though.

Traditional Audi buyers might also find the interior a bit lacking in terms of luxury. Yes, the materials are nice, but most of the dashboard is covered with screens (the passenger screen is optional) and piano-black plastic, both of which are fingerprint magnets. Also, while Audi's buttons and switches still feel tactile and satisfying to use, most features are controlled via the infotainment screen. Luckily, Audi acknowledged this recently and plans to pivot back to better interior materials and more buttons.

Cadillac's rival to the Audi Q6 isn't equipped with dual-motor AWD as standard, but it's also cheaper at $61,195. The Lyriq is a significantly larger SUV, too, providing a comfortable seating space for up to five people. Its trunk isn't as big, though, offering only 28 cubic feet of hauling capacity. And while the Lyriq's cabin features a massive 33-inch curved OLED screen, it's also dressed up in different textures for a more luxurious vibe. In keeping with its luxury pedigree, the Lyriq is supremely quiet inside thanks to noise-canceling tech.

The base Lyriq packs one electric motor on the rear axle, but with 365 horsepower, it's at least potent enough for a brisk 0 to 60 time of 5.7 seconds, according to Car and Driver. With a 102-kWh battery onboard, this model can travel up to 326 miles on a charge per EPA estimates. The Lyriq can't match the Q6 e-tron at the plug, though, topping out at a modest 190 kW. According to Cadillac, a 10-minute charge at this speed adds 86 miles of range.

If you want more oomph, Cadillac offers a dual-motor AWD model with 515 hp, which cuts the range down to 319 miles. There is also the high-performance Lyriq-V for people who want it all, armed with 615 hp and a 3.0-second sprint to 60 in our testing, but also a shorter 285-mile range.

The Macan Electric started a new era for Porsche, one where existing internal-combustion nameplates are used for their all-electric counterparts. More than that, though, the Macan Electric is now one of the most advanced EVs on the market, packing an 800-volt architecture, a large 100-kWh battery, a 270-kW charging rate, active suspension, and active aero.

Appropriate for a Porsche, the Macan Electric also caters to enthusiast drivers with the GTS (563 horsepower) and Turbo Electric (630 hp) models. Still, those are expensive, starting at $107,650 and $115,050, respectively. If all you want is a premium electric SUV with a Porsche badge on it, the base Macan Electric starts at $82,650. Sure, it only packs a rear 355-hp motor, but it still gets to 60 in a respectable 5.4 seconds.

With an EPA-estimated range of 332 miles, the base RWD model gets the most out of the battery, too. With a 324-mile range, the 402-hp Macan 4 isn't too far behind, and offers the benefit of all-wheel drive. The even peppier Macan 4S packs 509 hp, though it has a significantly shorter 290-mile range. (The GTS and Turbo models offer 294 and 293 miles of EPA-estimated range, respectively.)

Where the Macan differs from other electric SUVs is the driving experience, as it combines a poised, neutral chassis with communicative steering. It rides well, too, particularly the base model, which combines the standard air suspension with smaller 20-inch wheels. Unfortunately, the Macan Electric isn't very accommodating in the rear and has a tight overall cargo capacity of 22.1 cubic feet.

The aero-focused Hyundai Ioniq 9 makes you think twice about getting a premium five-seat SUV. Starting at around $61,000, it offers generous space in the first two rows and adds a useful third row for kids. Sliding the second-row seats reveals even more kneeroom at the back, with a useful 22-cubic-foot trunk behind the third row. Fold the third row flat, and the cargo capacity grows to 46 cubic feet, far surpassing that of five-seat electric SUVs.

The practical interior isn't the Ioniq 9's only strength. Its large 110.3-kWh battery gives the base rear-wheel-drive model an excellent 335 miles of range, while the 800-volt architecture lets it use 350-kW chargers. Yes, it only has 215 horsepower and needs 7.8 seconds to reach 60 (as tested by MotorTrend), but that's still quicker than the 2027 Palisade V6, which packs 287 hp and sprints to 60 in 7.9 seconds.

Both of the Ioniq 9's dual-motor versions also provide a 300-plus-mile range. The 303-hp Long Range model can travel 320 miles before the battery is depleted, while the 422-hp Performance model cuts that figure by only nine miles. The Ioniq 9 Performance is also genuinely quick in a straight line, ripping to 60 in 4.4 seconds.

The 2027 Tesla Model Y Premium RWD packs a relatively small 81.0-kWh battery, yet it can travel 355 miles with a full battery per EPA estimates. It does that by being very efficient with its use of electricity, meaning you'll spend less out of pocket per mile than in other EVs. The Model Y can also charge at a rate of up to 250 kW, restoring 182 miles of range in just 15 minutes.

The $47,630 Model Y Premium comes with a rear 304-horsepower motor as standard, which offers a brisk 0 to 60 time of 5.1 seconds, per Car and Driver. For $4,000 extra, you can add another motor on the front axle, which bumps the output to 397 hp and cuts the 0 to 60 sprint to 3.9 seconds and the EPA-estimated range to 327 miles. The base Model Y, which starts at $41,630, has a smaller battery and a 295-hp rear motor, but still extracts a good 321 miles on a charge.

The Model Y is fairly spacious inside and can carry 29.5 cubic feet of cargo. Buyers who want more room can now opt for the 7.0-inch-longer Model Y L, which adds a third row in the rear to accommodate up to six passengers. With the third row folded, it boasts a sizeable 38-cubic-foot trunk. Sold strictly in Premium trim, the Model Y L commands $63,630 for 332 miles of range.

With a base price of $85,885, the Rivian R1S is a significant step up from the Ioniq 9. Like its Hyundai rival, Rivian's SUV has three seating rows, with enough space for adults in the first two rows, but only for kids at the back. Luggage space behind the third row is on the snug side at 18 cubic feet (expanding to 49 folded), though a generous 10-cubic-foot frunk helps the R1S narrow the gap.

Where the R1S differs from its competition, however, is its off-road focus. Even the base model comes with a 533-horsepower dual-motor AWD powertrain and multiple drive modes that make the most out of it on slippery terrain. It's quick, too, dispatching 0 to 60 in 4.5 seconds. When equipped with the standard battery, the R1S has a 329-mile range, though the Long Range pack bumps this to 410 miles for $7,000.

The model range gets crazier as you go up the ladder; the R1S is also available in a 665-hp dual-motor guise, an 850-hp tri-motor beast that sprints to 60 in 2.9 seconds, and an unhinged quad-motor variant with 1,025 hp and a supercar-like 0 to 60 time of 2.6 seconds. Crucially, the Quad's four independent motors deliver precise, wheel-by-wheel torque control to maximize off-road traction.

Based on the Neue Klasse platform, the iX3 represents BMW's most consequential foray into the EV space, and the spec sheet immediately backs it up. Most notably, the massive 112-kWh battery pack, currently the only one available in the U.S., provides a class-leading 434-mile EPA-estimated range when equipped with 20-inch wheels and summer tires. The battery can also be topped up at a class-leading 400 kW, adding a whopping 230 miles of range in about 10 minutes. As a cherry on top, the iX3 50 xDrive comes as standard with a 463-hp dual-motor powertrain that allows a brisk 4.7-second 0 to 60 time.

But the Neue Klasse platform also brought some controversial changes. The iX3's interior is now almost fully digital, packing a 17.9-inch touchscreen that includes all climate controls and a 43.0-inch panoramic display under the windscreen. The steering wheel houses most of the buttons, but it's an awkward squircle shape that requires an acclimation period. Fortunately, despite being made mostly of sustainable materials, the iX3's interior feels premium. It's spacious for passengers, too, with a 30-cubic-foot trunk and a 2-cubic-foot frunk providing decent cargo storage.

The Lucid Gravity blurs the line between a minivan and an SUV, and we don't mean it in a bad way. It has by far the most practical cabin of all three-row electric SUVs, with ample space for adults in each row, multiple storage areas, and a large 48-cubic-foot trunk if you fold the third row. Keeping all seats in place leaves a smaller 15-cubic-foot cargo volume, but there is an additional 8-cubic-foot front trunk. The dashboard is unfortunately an all-digital affair, with a massive 34-inch curved display and a 12.6-inch touchscreen, but at least they are nicely integrated into the dash.

The brilliance of the Lucid Gravity goes beyond the practical interior. Based on an advanced 926-volt architecture, the Gravity supports charging speeds of up to 400 kW. It's efficient, too. The base $81,750 Gravity Touring model has a relatively small 89-kWh battery, yet it's still rated at 337 miles of range — impressive considering it has a dual-motor 560-horsepower drivetrain.

The $100,750 Gravity Grand Touring adds a larger battery for an EPA-estimated range of up to 450 miles. It also packs more oomph at 828 hp, enough for a blazing 0 to 60 sprint of 3.4 seconds. Lucid also offers a high-performance GT-S model with 1,070 hp that reaches 60 in just 3.1 seconds, but it also costs a steep $127,750.

The Cadillac Escalade IQ is all about big numbers. At a colossal 9,120 pounds, the Escalade IQ Luxury is heavy enough to escape the EPA's jurisdiction for official range testing. Still, according to Cadillac's estimates, the electric SUV behemoth can travel 465 miles before the battery depletes. Hardly surprising, as it comes with a gargantuan 205-kWh battery, enough for three compact SUVs with a ~250-mile range. Thanks to the 800-volt architecture, the Escalade IQ supports 350-kW DC charging.

Although it weighs significantly more than a fully equipped AWD pickup truck, the Escalade IQ is quick off the line, reaching 60 mph in a brisk 4.7 seconds. That's impressive considering the heft the 750-horsepower dual-motor AWD powertrain needs to deal with, but short of the 682-hp Escalade-V's 4.4-second sprint. Still, the Escalade IQ is significantly cheaper, with the base Luxury trim starting at $132,695, while the Escalade-V costs $172,195.

Now, sure, you don't get the sound from the Escalade-V's 6.2-liter supercharged V8 in the IQ, but if luxury is what you are after, the electric model delivers. Inside, the Escalade IQ treats occupants to palatial proportions across the first two rows, though you need the long-wheelbase IQL for an adult-friendly third row. Like the gas-powered Escalade, the IQ has a tech-forward interior with a massive 55-inch dashboard display, which is easy to use but offers no smartphone mirroring.

#5666. MD Defends 'The Pitt'; Dr. Glaucomflecken Talks Surprise Billing; Holiday Health PSA

The following contains links to social media websites including Bluesky, X, TikTok, Instagram, Facebook, and LinkedIn.

"You can't copyright the emergency room." An emergency physician defended the originality of "The Pitt," discussing a lawsuit filed by the widow and estate of "ER" creator Michael Crichton that alleges the shows are too similar.

Surviving childhood cancer inspired this pediatric resident to pursue her career in medicine.

#5665. Data Centers, AI, & the Growing Demand for Energy: Impacts, Risks, & Solutions

Learn how the rapid growth of data centers is affecting electricity bills, public health, communities, and the environment and what can be done about it.

Data centers, the physical infrastructure powering the rapid growth of artificial intelligence and our increasingly digital lives, are expanding at an unprecedented pace and scale around the world. This growth is creating enormous new demand for electricity and other resources, with consequences for communities and the environment. In the United States, where data center development is surging, unchecked growth could raise electricity bills for families and small businesses, strain power grids and water supplies, increase pollution, and slow progress toward a clean energy future.

Communities should not be left to bear the costs of this expansion without a meaningful voice in the decisions that affect them. People deserve transparency and a real say in whether and how data centers are built. The wealthy technology companies driving this growth should pay for the infrastructure their facilities require rather than shifting those costs onto the public, bring new clean energy to meet new electricity demand, and meet strong protections for public health and the environment.

Linked below are fact sheets, expert blogs, guides, and other resources that explore the impacts of data center development in the United States and around the world as well as solutions that can help ensure people and the planet remain at the center of the decisions ahead.

Asking ChatGPT a quick question might feel innocuous, but you might end up paying for it in the long run. The massive data centers that are popping up around the country to support the AI boom are using up enormous amounts of energy and water and creating noise and air pollution. NRDC’s Ben Schaefer, senior manager of strategic communications, and Jackson Morris, director of state power sector policy, dive into how these centers can impact nearby communities and your energy bill.

#5664. This Tractor Supply Mini-Excavator May Not Be The Smallest, But It Is One Of The Cutest

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

Remember playing with your Tonka trucks as a wee kiddo? If you grew up before tablets displaced little metal keepsakes, you ruled an imaginary work site, moving clumps of sand, dirt, and mud from one place to other spots. Granted, those other places were often your hair, shirt, and pants pockets. Oh, those were the days. Fret not, forever child — Yardmax has a tool that bridges the gap between practical equipment and a rideable Tonka-style toy.

It's the Yardmax YE0610, a 1,432-pound mini-excavator with a host of available attachments, from a claw to a grading bucket and rake.  Better yet, you might just be able to scoot on down to your local Tractor Supply and pick one up.

That said, Yardmax's diminutive earth mover isn't the smallest thing in the mini-excavator market. In fact, if you want to get crazy with your tiny tools, Honda and Komatsu once teamed up to make what may very well be the cutest little digging machine ever.

You've seen the big stuff. You know, the massive, earth-moving heavy construction equipment required to build a modern highway. Monstrous bulldozers, cold milling machines, and long-necked dinosaur-ish excavators are all part of the process. Some of these dirt-munching excavators can get properly huge, with the biggest examples tipping the scales at over 2 million pounds. Yes, you read that right.

Other excavators, however, go for more of a park-it-in-your-garage footprint. That's what the Yardmax YE0610 mini-excavator is all about. At just 27.5 inches wide, the wee digging machine's width takes up considerably less floor space than a common go-kart. Even with the canopy installed on the YE0610-575003 model, the mini-excavator is 62.4 inches tall and 1,674 pounds. Opt for the topless base YE0610, and that weight drops to 1,432 pounds. Not exactly a 2-million-pound earth mover.

Okay, so it's cute. Can it dig? Yardmax asserts the YE0610 can dig up to 47.2 inches deep, or just under four feet. Then there are the attachments. Depending on the model, the Yardmax can use a claw, grading bucket, or rake for your digging, filling, clearing, and leveling tasks. As far as power goes, the mini-excavator features a 306-cc engine capable of hustling the little machine at a cautious 1-mph pace. As expected, the excavator can rotate 360 degrees, regardless of track position.

This wee workhorse might be one of the wildest things Tractor Supply sells. Then again, it might not be. After all, this is a retailer that also offers a teeny tiny minibike to awaken your inner child.

As of this writing, the Yardmax YE0610, complete with its removable canopy, grading bucket, and rake, is for sale at Tractor Supply for $6,999.99. Want to save a couple hundred bucks on your backyard digging machine? The same setup is available at Walmart for $6,799. Of course, if you'd rather go in another direction entirely, Tractor Supply also sells a Power Wheels-esque mini truck. Your mini truck won't likely do any excavating for you, though.

Here's the thing, though: the Yardmax YE0610 isn't the smallest thing on the mini-excavator market. Croba, a sub-brand of MechMaxx, produces a MEC06 mini-excavator that weighs 87 pounds less than the Yardmax and undercuts the Tractor Supply price for the YE0610 by nearly $2,000, though its max digging depth falls shy of the YE0610 by 3.9 inches.

Like a shrinking set of nesting dolls, things can get even smaller. Komatsu, a Japanese heavy machinery manufacturer, joined forces with Honda in 2022 to produce an unbearably cute rideable excavator for the Japanese market. The result was the Komatsu PC01E-1, a 750-pound electric micro excavator with unmistakable toy aisle looks.

Better yet, the PC01E-1 falls just shy of the 771.6-pound max load capacity of Japanese mini trucks. So, go ahead, load your pint-sized electric excavator into your tiny truck.

#5663. Outer Spaces takes solar beyond the rooftop with integrated backyard energy system

Residential solar is finding a new place to call home: the backyard.

Outer Spaces, a new company founded by the entrepreneurs behind outdoor living brand Outer, is introducing a solar-powered pavilion that combines photovoltaic generation, battery storage and usable outdoor living space. The company’s first product, OS1, can be designed for multiple functions, including an outdoor kitchen, office, gym or gathering space, while generating electricity and providing backup power for the home.

Outer Spaces is planning to produce outdoor structures that integrate solar PV and energy storage. Credit: Outer Spaces

The California-based company is positioning OS1 as a new category of residential energy infrastructure that goes beyond conventional rooftop arrays and garage-mounted batteries. It builds on the growing interest in solar structures that also serve a secondary purpose.

OS1 combines a solar pavilion with a finished outdoor power deck. Solar panels integrated into the overhead structure generate electricity, while a thin battery storage system is housed beneath the deck. According to Outer Spaces, the combination allows the structure to produce energy throughout the day and provide backup power during outages.

Rather than competing exclusively with traditional rooftop solar, OS1 offers homeowners an additional way to use available property for energy production, particularly when roof aesthetics, space limitations, or homeowner preferences become obstacles to conventional installations.

Outer Spaces describes OS1 as a modular system designed for rapid on-site assembly, with minimal to no excavation required. The company says installation avoids traditional construction steps such as concrete pouring and lumber cutting. Once the structure is assembled, a licensed electrician then connects its energy system to the customer’s home.

The product’s first availability is scheduled for 2027, beginning in Southern California and select additional markets; pricing is expected to be announced in early 2027.

Solar canopies and pergolas have increasingly incorporated features such as EV charging, lighting, fans and weather protection, giving homeowners additional reasons to invest in solar outside the traditional rooftop model.

Outer Spaces represents another potential avenue for expanding residential solar adoption through design-driven products. The opportunity will ultimately depend on pricing, installation economics, permitting requirements and the system’s measurable energy performance.

Outer Spaces plans to showcase OS1 publicly for the first time at RE+ 2026, Nov. 16 to 19, in Las Vegas.

#5662. Is Tesla The Apple Of The EV Industry?

Tesla is an interesting company at an interesting phase of the electric vehicle transition. Well, it has always been an interesting company in this transition. However, lately, as the industry has moved quickly into a new phase, I have been wondering where Tesla will sit amidst it in the coming years.

While there are now hundreds and hundreds of electric vehicle models on the market and EV share of new vehicle sales have risen to 32% globally (or 23% if we only count full electrics/BEVs), Tesla sells only two models but remains the second best selling BEV producer in the world. It remains solidly ahead of other automakers and only trails Chinese giant BYD (or also Geely if we throw in PHEVs).

In the US, where there’s much less EV competition (especially because of Chinese EVs basically being blocked from entering the market), Tesla’s share of EV sales is above 50%.

The Apple iPhone holds about 20–22% of the global smartphone market, and around 58% of the US smartphone market. While that’s a bit higher than Tesla’s shares, it’s quite similar. And considering that Apple just sells that one phone (but in a few different versions) and Tesla just sells the Model 3 and Model Y, it’s hard to not see a comparison.

As EV sales have been picking up in parts of the world, or at least reaching portions of the market in the USA, it seems to me that a lot of people choose Tesla because they’ve heard it’s the EV brand. Tesla has long been the face of the EV transition in most of the world, and even considered the only “real” option. I know of many buyers in the US who just went straight to Tesla without looking at other options. It’s a bit like how many people will only consider and look at Apple for phones. However, how much will that change in the coming years? Will Tesla’s dominance fade further (it’s already down from about 80% of the market in the US to a bit more than 50%) as more and more options come to market, or will it hold it’s special position as a first mover, a tech leader, and an EV-only carmaker?

One more interesting issue here is the matter of “Full Self Driving” — which only comes as “Supervised” at the moment, but Tesla is itching to make a fully hands-off, eyes-off option for Tesla owners. Could Tesla achieve a leading role for years again of having the only fully hands-off, eyes-off, self-driving consumer cars on the road? Or is it not going to beat other automakers to market with a self-driving feature that takes liability for any accidents away from the human operating the car and allows them to sleep, watch movies, work, etc. in the car? This has been hotly debated for about a decade, with Tesla nearly a decade late on Elon Musk’s targets for this, but the company is now operating robotaxis in a few cities and ramping up production of the Cybercab. Risky or not, is Tesla close to taking on liability for humans using FSD in their own cars? And if it does take that step, will that make demand for Tesla cars so high that Tesla sees its EV market share increase significantly again?

I know many people have strong opinions on these matters — on both sides of the debate. Some are certain Tesla is going to rise again and will release true Full Self Driving broadly soon, or at least long before other automakers. Some are certain Tesla’s vision-only approach is fatally flawed and Tesla will never take on full liability for Tesla owners using FSD. I don’t have enough insight or expertise to say one thing is absolutely certain and the other is not, but I do think the FSD matter is quite important to Tesla remaining “the Apple of the EV industry” — even potentially without taking on accident liability. Even with things staying as they are, I wonder if Tesla could remain the dominant EV player in the US and some other markets just because of its early reputation, branding, and 21st century marketing. We shall see.

Zach is tryin' to help society help itself one word at a time. He spends most of his time here on CleanTechnica as its editor-in-chief and CEO. Zach is recognized globally as an electric vehicle, solar energy, and energy storage expert. He has presented about electric vehicles and renewable energy at conferences in India, the UAE, Ukraine, Poland, Germany, the Netherlands, the USA, Canada, and Curaçao.

#5661. Prescriptions Down 25%, Overdoses Cut in Half: What Stopped a Gabapentinoid Surge?

— Study identifies three interventions that may have influenced pregabalin use

High-profile media coverage of the harms associated with the gabapentinoid pregabalin (Lyrica) and tighter safeguards coincided with fewer prescriptions and overdoses in Australia, a retrospective study showed.

Following a media campaign, tighter dispensing rules, and boxed warnings, pregabalin prescriptions and overdoses dropped significantly below projected trends -- falling by 25% and 47%, respectively -- reported Rose Cairns, PhD, of the University of Sydney, and co-authors in The BMJ.

This was a reversal from a steep upward trajectory that had persisted since 2013, the researchers noted. The shift corresponded to ongoing monthly reductions in prescriptions of 0.7% (95% CI 0.5-0.9) and in poison center overdose calls of 1.6% (95% 1.0-2.3).

Australia historically has had a big problem with pregabalin use and overdose, and many other countries face similar issues, Cairns noted.

"Importantly, the interventions that were associated with a decrease in harms included things like raising public awareness of the risks via the media and boxed warnings. These relatively 'soft' interventions can still have an impact," she told MedPage Today.

The findings are "really positive, but we think use of pregabalin is still far too high," Cairns continued. "There's a lot of off-label use for conditions with minimal evidence to back that up. And while high-risk use has decreased, we really need to continue to minimize co-use with opioids, which has been shown to increase the risk of death."

Gabapentinoids -- pregabalin and gabapentin -- were originally developed as antiseizure drugs. Subsequent indications have expanded to include neuropathic pain, postherpetic neuralgia, and fibromyalgia.

"Despite this relatively narrow range of approved indications, gabapentinoids are considered 'blockbuster' drugs," Cairns and colleagues wrote. "High-income countries have the highest rates of gabapentinoid consumption, while low- and middle-income countries have had the highest consumption growth in recent years. Most of this consumption is driven by off-label use for non-registered indications."

In the U.S., pregabalin is scheduled under the Controlled Substances Act. Gabapentin is not scheduled at the federal level but eight states have enacted legislation to classify gabapentin as a schedule V controlled substance.

In 2019, the FDA warned that respiratory depression or death may occur in patients taking pregabalin or gabapentin with opioid pain medicines or other drugs that depress the central nervous system. Despite this, co-prescribing of gabapentinoids and opioids persists.

In the U.K., one-third of substance use deaths involve a gabapentinoid, predominantly pregabalin, and deaths involving gabapentinoids have more than doubled from 2018 to 2022, Cairns and co-authors noted.

The study in Australia is "timely and rigorous, but these figures warrant caution," noted David Juurlink, MD, PhD, of the Sunnybrook Health Sciences Centre in Toronto, in an accompanying editorial. "The ecological design cannot establish causation, and poison center calls are an imperfect measure of poisoning incidence, influenced by reporting practices and patterns of healthcare use," he pointed out.

Pregabalin should be used more selectively, Juurlink argued. "Although it can provide meaningful relief in some patients with neuropathic pain or fibromyalgia, most do not achieve substantial pain relief," he wrote.

"For other painful conditions, the evidence is even less encouraging," Juurlink added. "Randomized trials and meta-analyses have found no convincing evidence of benefit for low back pain or sciatica, conditions for which the drug is nevertheless frequently prescribed. Pregabalin's commercial success, in other words, has far outstripped its demonstrated clinical utility."

To evaluate pregabalin use and misuse in Australia, Cairns and colleagues evaluated dispensing claims and poison center overdose data from 2016 to 2024, focusing on three key events: widespread media reporting about the harms of pregabalin in December 2018, tighter prescribing restrictions after April 2020, and the introduction of boxed warnings in February 2021.

The researchers randomly sampled 10% of Australians with drug subsidies and 50% of calls placed to the country's largest poison control center. During the study period, 227,864 people were dispensed 3,441,851 pregabalin prescriptions, and 4,871 pregabalin overdose calls were reported.

Use and overdoses peaked in 2018 before plateauing and declining. Dispensing dropped significantly at three time points: in December 2018 (corresponding to high-profile press coverage), in May 2020 (after tighter prescribing restrictions were introduced), and in December 2021 (after boxed warnings were in effect).

The findings are observational, the researchers acknowledged. The study analyzed subsidized prescriptions only and poisons center data may be subject to reporting bias, they added. The researchers also could not determine patients' access to pain treatments.

#5660. Europe’s Hydrogen Strategy Should Begin With the Industrial Customer

A European fertiliser manufacturer buys ammonia. Its immediate concern is getting the right product, at the right quality and price, with reliable deliveries. The manufacturer may produce ammonia using hydrogen made on its own site, buy hydrogen through a pipeline, or import ammonia made somewhere else. The last choice preserves much of the downstream industrial work but creates no demand for additional hydrogen production or pipelines inside Europe. That distinction belongs at the start of Europe’s hydrogen strategy.

The European Commission has reopened the strategy for consultation, with submissions due November 23. The Commission acknowledges that renewable and low-carbon hydrogen production and use have grown more slowly than expected. Europe’s public investment decisions now need a closer look at the industries expected to use hydrogen, the products they make, and the routes by which those products can be supplied competitively. The consultation is open until November 23, 2026.

I submitted a demand-led assessment to the Commission on November 11. My TFIE Strategy Briefing explains the industrial argument, and the open research report documents the model and its assumptions.

Europe’s existing hydrogen market is primarily a market for industrial chemicals. ACER estimated EU consumption of about 7.4 million tons in 2024, including 4.2 million tons for petroleum refining and 1.9 million tons for ammonia. Passenger cars, building heat and power generation account for very little of that established market. Refining faces structural decline as petroleum demand changes, while fertiliser and chemical producers can keep manufacturing products even when some upstream inputs come from elsewhere.

Steelmaking also has choices that cannot be captured by a single hydrogen-demand target. Producers can use recycled scrap, purchase reduced iron made where electricity is cheaper, or use hydrogen-based direct reduction where its economics work. The equipment, feedstock quality and production location determine the actual hydrogen requirement. Industrial heat offers direct electrical options for many duties. Transport electrification cuts the amount of liquid fuel that must be produced in the first place. These choices determine hydrogen demand long before anyone chooses a pipeline route.

Electricity costs expose why the industrial comparison matters. Electrolysis consuming 55 kWh/kg uses €3.30 of electricity per kilogram at €60/MWh, and €5.50 at €100/MWh. Those prices exclude the electrolyser, finance, maintenance, compression and delivery. An industrial customer pays for the finished product, not for the complexity of the process used to manufacture it.

The new assessment compares qualifying green ammonia imported into Europe at modelled 2050 delivered costs of €755–839/t with European ammonia made using on-site hydrogen at about €1,222/t. The €383–467/t gap puts a substantial price on choosing the domestic route in this model. Emissions certification, quality, reliability and supply concentration belong in the procurement appraisal, alongside the cost and strategic value of retaining European upstream production. They do not turn a forecast of ammonia demand into a forecast of domestic hydrogen demand.

Industrial policy has reasons to support European production beyond the lowest immediate price. Domestic facilities provide employment, technical capabilities and supply security. The right decision is to identify those benefits, calculate the extra cost and state who will pay it. Hydrogen production quotas bury that choice in a volume target and risk making European industrial products less competitive.

The infrastructure consequence follows. A pipeline needs industrial customers who can afford delivered hydrogen. A capacity reservation or a project announcement provides neither a binding purchase agreement nor a business case for long-term utilisation. Germany’s hydrogen-network reservations and the Netherlands’ lower demand projections already make that difference worth examining. Europe should assess its planned networks against the industrial customers likely to remain after process and sourcing choices change.

European network planners already run scenarios covering industrial activity, technology and hydrogen supply. My November submission proposes adding direct competition between ways of supplying equivalent industrial products and transport services, including the location of production. The resulting hydrogen requirements then become inputs to infrastructure appraisal instead of predetermined outputs of hydrogen policy.

The next step is practical. Keep a proposed pipeline’s design, cost, capacity and planned opening date unchanged. Assess that same investment first under a policy-aligned case that includes existing hydrogen mandates, and then under a separate economic-competition case in which industrial buyers can select other emissions-qualified production routes. Publish projected utilisation, binding customers, delivered costs, tariffs and public exposure. After seeing the downside in the original proposal, compare smaller pipes, staged construction, local hydrogen supply or imported industrial inputs. Redesigning the pipeline at the outset of every scenario hides the risk of the investment actually under consideration.

The accompanying research uses a reproducible Python model, an evidence workbook and an interactive dashboard. Readers can change assumptions about industrial imports, technology and production costs and see how the modelled hydrogen requirement responds. In the Strong pathway with central costs, selected European industrial markets and transport services require 1.887 million tons of physical hydrogen in 2050. Halving the assumed import capacity for industrial products raises that result to 3.206 million tons. These figures describe selected markets and exclude hydrogen embodied in imported finished products; they are not a forecast of total EU demand.

A competitive industrial economy can still need hydrogen for chemicals and some other applications. It needs production and pipelines sized for customers, with any extra capacity supported by a stated security or industrial rationale. The Commission’s renewed strategy should begin with Europe’s industrial products and services, then calculate the hydrogen system worth paying for.

Read the original TFIE Strategy Briefing, the open European hydrogen assessment, and explore the interactive dashboard, evidence workbook and reproducible Python model.

#5659. Was The Tesla Roadster Reveal Realistic? Engineering Explained Fact Checked It

Unless you've been living under a rock, it's unlikely that you've missed Tesla's wild Roadster event, or at least haven't heard about it. In all honesty, the reveal (or rather re-reveal) was actually pretty insane. While the upside-down stunt was already accomplished by the McMurtry Spéirling, the rest of the show (regardless of whether you're a fan of Elon) left enthusiasts in genuine awe. The car flew, did a kickflip, and even managed a crazy 0-60 time (without rollout) of 0.81 seconds.

If only there was someone with a whiteboard who could break down the physics and legitimacy of the new Roadster and its many engineering feats. That's precisely what Jason Fenske from YouTube's Engineering Explained set out to do. Because specifications of the Roadster aren't available yet, including weight distribution, dimensions, and so on, Fenske had to improvise with assumptions. But the gist is: the new Tesla Roadster reveal appears to be legit, though with a few caveats.

Fenske begins by analyzing the Roadster's fan-assisted aerodynamics. For the Roadster to stay upside down (as seen in the demo), the estimated pressure differential is around 11.5 kilopascals (kPa), — an 11.5% vaccum — achieved with help from fans that suck air out of a small area underneath the car that's sealed using deployable skirts.

That 11.5 kPa is the pressure difference between the top of the car (1 atmosphere, or approximately 100 kPa) and the bottom of the car (88.5 kPa), arrived at using the downforce figures from Tesla's demo and Fenske's assumption of the Roadster's skirt area. While he reckons the system could theoretically generate up to 90,000 kilograms of downforce (if that 88.5 kPa is brought down to zero, i.e., 100% vacuum), the car only needed an 11.5% vacuum to stay upside down.

Next up, Fenske breaks down the Roadster's brief flight and kickflip demonstration. For that, the mass flow rate of the 250 kilograms of available nitrogen (stored at 10,000 pounds per square inch in the two storage tanks) is determined using rocket science and a few assumptions. Dividing the available mass of nitrogen (250 kilos) by the mass flow rate (37 kg/s) reveals that the Roadster (in Rocket spec) can hover in the air for about seven seconds. Fenske reckons that both stunts are possible, considering each took around four seconds to complete. Whether Tesla used larger storage tanks or a lighter shell is debatable, but the demo appears to be plausible.

As for the 0-60 times, Fenske starts with the Base Roadster, which is yet to be confirmed as a production model. In any case, plugging the variables (most of which are assumptions) into a fancy equation reveals that the Base Roadster can achieve a maximum acceleration of 0.99 g. This translates to a 0-60 time of around 2.76 seconds. On the Performance variant, which comes equipped with the fans, Fenske arrives at an estimated 2.3 g and 1.2 seconds for its 0-60. However, he does mention caveats, as variables like the friction coefficient and pressure distribution can influence the final number.

To see whether the Thrust version can actually run a 0-60 of 0.81 seconds, he connects the model's acceleration of 3.37 g to the mass flow rate of nitrogen (37 kg/s). According to Fenske, if you only need around 40 kilos of nitrogen to deliver 1 g for 1.0 seconds, the 200-liter storage tank has enough and more to hit 3.37 g. As for the Rocket variant, Fenske estimates a 0-60 time of 0.5 seconds, pulling a whopping 5.5 g based on his assumptions.

At the end of the day, this event was a great marketing stunt. And that's pretty much how Fenske sees it, because the Roadster's upside-down trick has a few real-world nuances to consider. For instance, the skirts are a wear item, as are the fans. Using the McMurtry as an example, he explains that debris can still enter the fans no matter how efficiently the system filters out the air. Also, the downforce levels depend on the surface, and the demo uses an ideal setup, according to Fenske, which is far from the typical unevenness of actual road surfaces. So real-world performance may be different.

Perhaps the bigger question is: Will this Roadster ever end up in the hands of a consumer? After all, we have seen many of Musk's promises never materialize. There was the promise of a 500-mile Cybertruck and Model S. Those never happened, and neither did many of the previous Roadster's claims. That car was supposed to have four seats and a 620-mile range. The new one is a two-seater (or single-seater, depending on the model), and Tesla's VP of vehicle engineering, Lars Moravy, told Top Gear it will end up with only around 310 to 320 miles of real-world range. Heck, the new car isn't even really a roadster — it's a T-top.

Musk claims the production Roadster will do even better than what was shown in the event, and Tesla says it will enter production in 2028. But as Fenske points out in the video, "Tesla says things and then they do not do those things ... You really don't know with this company until the product actually ships." So let's leave it at that.

#5658. Major Change to Prostate Cancer Screening Guidelines

— ACS now recommends PSA testing every 2 years for average-risk men ages 50 to 69

Shifting gears on prostate cancer screening, the American Cancer Society (ACS) now recommends screening with prostate-specific antigen (PSA) every 2 years for average-risk men ages 50 to 69.

Since the release of its previous guideline 16 years ago, the ACS had recommended that average-risk men consult with a physician before deciding for themselves whether to undergo a screening PSA test.

The new ACS recommendations also strengthened guidelines for men at high risk for prostate cancer, recommending annual screening beginning at age 45 for Black men and those with a strong family history of the disease. The organization previously recommended that those men discuss the pros and cons of screening with a physician at age 40 or 45.

The changes reflect the accumulation of stronger evidence that screening PSA tests reduce prostate cancer mortality and diagnosis of late-stage disease, according to the updated guidelines from Robert Smith, PhD, ACS senior vice president for early cancer detection science, and colleagues, appearing in CA: A Cancer Journal for Clinicians.

"We've just reached a point in our understanding of prostate cancer, especially that [screening] can reduce the risk of being diagnosed with metastatic disease and dying of prostate cancer," Smith told MedPage Today. "We have good protocols for avoiding harms."

Smith noted that the U.S. Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) has not updated their prostate cancer screening guidelines since 2018. For average-risk men, the USPSTF recommends discussion-backed decisions on PSA testing for men ages 55 to 69.

"I cannot tell you what [their recommendations] would be if they updated them today, but they did not have the data in 2018 that we had in 2025 with the update of the European trial data showing the endurance of the mortality reduction," said Smith.

The update aligns with recent evidence supporting PSA screening for prostate cancer as "one of the most important steps men can take to protect their health," said Ash Tewari, MD, of the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai in New York City.

"We have strong evidence that PSA testing can reduce the risk of dying from prostate cancer, and advances in MRI and other diagnostic tools are helping us identify potentially dangerous cancers while avoiding unnecessary procedures," he said. "Knowing your risk, getting screened regularly, and having these conversations early can save lives."

With the update, the ACS takes a clear position on prostate cancer screening, said Mark Ashamalla, MD, of Episcopal Health Services in New York City.

"For years, the ACS largely focused on encouraging men to discuss the pros and cons of PSA screening with their doctors," said Ashamalla. "Now they're taking a much clearer position and actually recommending regular screening. For men at average risk, that means a PSA blood test every 2 years between ages 50 and 69, while Black men and those with a strong family history should begin annual screening at age 45. This is a significant shift and reflects growing evidence that regular screening can reduce the risk of advanced prostate cancer and save lives."

"The goal isn't to find and treat every prostate cancer," he added. "It's to catch the dangerous ones early enough to make a difference. I think that's the biggest takeaway from these new guidelines."

By way of the guidelines, the ACS also recommends:

The updated guidelines are supportive of the recent trend toward active surveillance as the primary option for men with low-risk prostate cancer.

"It's important to recognize that prostate cancer screening has evolved over the past 30 years," said Smith. "More and more people appreciate that very worrisome harms associated with therapy can be avoided, especially if a man is diagnosed with prostate cancer that is not aggressive. Active treatment should be avoided until such time as it appears that the disease has become aggressive. Some men may be diagnosed with a prostate cancer that's not very aggressive, and they will live with it in that state for the rest of their lives. [They can] avoid harms that are quite diminishing to quality of life."

"On the other hand, if a man's prostate cancer becomes more aggressive, and treatment is necessary, then the process of active surveillance will at least have postponed it for a number of years, allowing men to not have to live with those [treatment-related] side effects," said Smith.

#5657. Diesel Prices Used To Be Cheaper Than Gas - Here's Why That Changed

Many Americans are not particularly happy about the country's soaring gas prices. Currently, the national average for diesel is $6.06 a gallon, and gasoline is at $3.87, according to AAA. While tensions in the Middle East have some part to play in increasing global crude oil prices, diesel being more expensive than gasoline is not something new in the country.

In fact, "On-highway diesel fuel prices have been higher than regular-grade gasoline prices, on a dollar-per-gallon basis, almost continuously since September 2004," according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration (EIA). Before that diesel was typically cheaper than gas, except during winter months when demand was high, as the fuel is commonly used in home heating.

Relatively high demand across Europe, China, India, and the United States is one of the reasons why diesel got more expensive than gasoline over the years. Beyond the usual supply-demand dynamics, other variables, like the push for low-sulfur diesels, also raised the fuel's production and distribution costs, due to the additional refining involved. Another contributing factor, per the EIA, is the federal excise tax for on-highway diesel, which is 6 cents a gallon higher than gasoline. Throw geopolitics and supply constraints into the mix, and you start to see why prices are so high.

The rate at which diesel prices rose when compared to AAA's data from the same time last year is quite staggering. While gasoline's national average was $3.08 a gallon, diesel's was at $3.76, meaning diesel jumped more than 60% during that period. And that's despite the U.S. running a diesel surplus and refineries churning out at near-maximum capacity with 97% utilization. So, why are diesel prices so high?

Despite less than 3% of U.S. passenger cars using diesel, the economy, whether it's shipping, logistics, agriculture, or construction, relies heavily on diesel. Compared to gasoline, diesel is a bit more inelastic, meaning price changes won't necessarily impact its demand. "People still drive a lot when gas prices go up, but consumers do vote with their pocketbook, and they will cut back on trips and stuff," says Gregg Ibendahl, an associate professor of agricultural economics at Kansas State University (via Scientific American). "When it comes to diesel fuel, though, it's not that way at all," Ibendahl continues, "Diesel price is $2.50 higher than what it was in spring, but that's not going to stop a single farmer from running their combine out there."

According to another EIA report, high crude oil prices and reduced refining activity in Russia, China, and the Middle East are part of the reason for the rising diesel prices in recent months. Ukraine targeted many of Russia's refineries, China suspended its fuel exports to protect its domestic stocks, and the Strait of Hormuz ... well, you know what's happening there. This naturally increases demand for exports from the United States, as the country is one of the largest producers of refined oil products in the world. If you weren't aware, the U.S. exports about 1.5 million barrels of diesel per day. Domestic consumption is around 3.6 million barrels per day, and the country refines roughly 5.3 million barrels per day, according to the American Action Forum. So, why not cut the exports like China did? Won't more domestic supply reduce diesel prices?

An export ban won't necessarily make things any better, reckons the aforementioned American Action Forum. According to its report, while an export ban may initially cool wholesale prices in the Gulf Coast (where major U.S. diesel production is concentrated), lower prices may not translate to other parts of the country because of logistics and infrastructure constraints.

In the case of New England, for example, it's much cheaper to import diesel from Canada than to source it domestically. The West Coast, Hawaii, and Alaska also import diesel to supplement their domestic supply. A sustained ban would eventually force U.S. refiners to cut back on production as they might face trouble finding options to absorb the excess supply. If refineries reduce production, it would drive up prices of all petroleum products, including diesel, which defeats the whole point of having the ban in the first place.

If global tensions continue and supply constraints remain, it's unlikely that diesel prices will come down. President Trump claiming "We don't need a deal with Iran" is certainly not a positive sign. Hopefully, the Democrats, now having won the midterms, could perhaps turn things around.

#5656. Ask a Scientist: Who Has a Voice in Clean Air Protections?

If developers and planners decide to site an industrial chemical plant next door to your home, you’d want the chance to speak up about it. Soliciting input from the communities and individuals affected by pollution is an essential part of how environmental and public health laws are enforced. Under the federal Clean Air Act, large polluting facilities like power plants, chemical manufacturers, and refineries need to have a building permit, and every permitting decision must go through a “notice and comment” process. Although this is a federal law, it’s largely implemented by state, regional, and local agencies. And as a new UCS analysis shows, how much influence people have on the air permitting process varies greatly depending on where they live.

Darya Minovi leads fair access research for the Center for Science and Democracy at UCS. She is the lead author of Community Access to Clean Air Decisionmaking: Analysis of State Engagement in Title V Permitting, a new report that looks state-by-state at how people can make their voices heard.

AAS: What inspired you to do this analysis?

Darya Minovi: Last year, our team did an analysis of how the Trump administration is limiting public input on regulations andalso developed a set of recommendations for how government agencies should ensure the public gets to participate. With the administration very clearly side-stepping or ignoring these public processes, we did not, to put it lightly, expect the federal government to be a willing recipient of our research and guidance.

During the Biden administration, the White House Office of Management and Budget (OMB) did a lot of work to assess and expand meaningful public participation in government decisions. That was essentially all thrown away when President Trump was inaugurated. After consulting with colleagues and partners, we wanted to know: in this vacuum where the federal government isn’t interested in making progress, could we work with states to advance the public’s ability to participate in environmental policies?

AAS: Why does this participation matter, and how did you end up focusing on the air pollution permitting process?

DM: When you look at the core principles of environmental justice, meaningful engagement is central. People have the right to understand what’s happening in their communities and ideally be part of shaping those decisions. They need the opportunity to speak out and be respectfully listened to. People’s lived experience is expertise that can and should inform policy decisions. That’s democracy in action. At the federal level, environmental justice has been targeted and dismantled, but some states still value these

One specific environmental program where public participation is relevant is in Title V of the federal Clean Air Act (CAA), which covers operating permits for facilities that generate air pollution. This includes power plants, refineries, trash incinerators, and factories that manufacture chemicals—under the law, these are often called “major sources,” and there are very specific legal requirements about public participation. It’s one of the main ways air pollution gets regulated. But even though CAA is a federal law, it’s largely state and local agencies that carry out this permit process.

When a facility owner or company wants to apply for a permit to operate, they must submit a permit application to the relevant state, regional, or local agency. The agency writes a draft permit, which must be posted publicly in some way. The CAA requires agencies to accept public comments on the draft permit for at least 30 days and, if requested by the public, hold a hearing. Agencies also have a legal obligation to respond to substantive comments. These are very clear steps laid out in the law. It’s a high bar for participation, but we wanted to better understand how it works in practice.

What we found in our analysis is that the agencies with the authority to issue permits will approve Title V air permits unless there’s some clear problem with legal compliance. But the public can use that “notice and comment” process to slow down and influence what the facility will actually be permitted to do.

However, some agencies treat the public comment process as a set of boxes to check off. The law isn’t specific about meaningful engagement practices beyond public comment, so our analysis also looked at practices, programs, guidance, and laws at the state level, beyond what’s required by the federal government to enhance public participation in air permitting.

AAS: How did you do this analysis, and what did you find?

DM: We wanted to get the answers to three big questions.

We started with what I’ll call “desktop research”—reviewing agency websites and laws as though we were residents looking to navigate the process. All 50 states do have at least a website with some information. In general, agencies are posting draft permits and accepting public comments, as the CAA requires. But there are big differences in how that happens.

Public hearings aren’t very frequent and usually happen only if there’s a critical mass of people demanding them. We also reviewed nearly 90 permits from 35 different states—the public notices are often very technical, not easily readable by a layperson, and they’ll say things like “comments are due 30 days from posting date” instead of giving the actual dated deadline.

Very few states translate these notices into languages other than English. And we assessed whether the notices use “plain language” best practices—defining scientific terms, using white space and bullet points—and found that most states aren’t integrating these effectively. Minnesota and Michigan stand out as providing clear, readable information, and that’s largely the result of advocacy by local groups there.

We found that while 42 states had some resources describing how people can participate in permitting, the quality varied significantly. By and large permitting webpages and resources seem better designed for regulated industries, not the general public.

Ultimately, a public notice is only useful if you can find it, read it, and understand what it means and what you’re supposed to do to get involved!

We also wanted to know: do the comments make a difference? If they’re just going to get collected and sit on a shelf, that’s less meaningful than if agencies answer them or make actual changes to the permit in response. It’s very rare for agencies to deny a permit, but there are ways for public comments to have an impact—correcting errors, adding monitoring requirements, and strengthening enforcement.

AAS: You reached out to states to learn more. What did you find?

DM: Talking directly to these state agencies was an important part of our research. We built an 88-question survey, based on our September 2025 guidance, to ask agencies how they provide information, what they do to encourage participation, and what resources they have to carry the work out.

While we sent that survey to every state, only half the states responded in full, with 3 more responding partially. A few states said they intended to respond but didn’t, while others declined to participate or failed to respond entirely. That itself speaks to the capacity and, perhaps, interest these agencies have.

The states’ answers varied. Of the 28 states we heard back from, only 18 said they post responses to comments online, and only 13 could give us an example of when public comments resulted in a change to a permit.

At least 11 states have laws that go beyond what’s required in the CAA, and those laws often center environmental justice or cumulative impacts. States with these laws tended to have more readily available information on how to participate, as well as more resources and funding devoted to public outreach. Having these laws—or at least guidelines—in place does seem to create agency-wide changes to enhance public engagement.

Meaningful engagement takes time, money, and actual staff to manage the process. Only 20 of the agencies we spoke to provide training on public participation or environmental justice for staff, and only 16 have actual full-time staff dedicated to the process.

Unfortunately, the federal government is pushing in the opposite direction. Aaron Szabo, the (now former) director of the air-quality office at the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency, issued a memo urging states to expedite the Title V permitting process and rapidly review permits—saying explicitly that agencies don’t get many comments, so they don’t really matter.

DM: I was surprised by how much variability there was between and even within states. Some might have a lot of resources explaining how to participate in air permitting, but it’s a confusing website. Others might translate public notices in other languages but have no other information about how to participate.

While there’s a lot of room for improvement, there are some instructive examples too. For example, North Dakota has explicit guidance on how to write an effective public comment, with examples of common mistakes to avoid. Connecticut and Wisconsin have specific “how to” webpages to help people make comments or request a hearing. South Carolina has a webpage specifically entitled “Air Quality Decisions” that lays out what decision was made and posts the comments made in response to the draft permit. Ohio was the only state that got an “A” grade for readability of a public notice, with most states falling in the B/C range.

Every state can do better, of course, and we have a set of recommendations for how these agencies can improve. Governors, state lawmakers, and agency heads have a role to play here—certainly with funding for agencies and laws, but also in how they direct their agencies to carry this process out. Using public health data, translating notices into culturally appropriate languages and presenting them in plain language, making sure there’s fulltime staff devoted to public participation—these are just some of the steps to make participating in the air permitting process more meaningful, so that people not only have their voices heard, but actually have influence on decisions that shape air quality in their communities.

Courtesy of The Equation, UCS. By Seth D. Michaels.

#5655. Secure Solar Futures set to install 313-kW array on Virginia elementary school

Secure Solar Futures, a commercial solar contractor, will build a 313-kW solar power system at Preston Park Elementary School in Roanoke, Virginia. Construction of the system is expected to start later this year at no cost to the Roanoke Schools through a 25-year power purchase agreement with Secure Solar Futures.

A total of 531 bifacial solar panels manufactured by VSUN Solar in Houston, Texas, will be installed on the rooftop of the school, and the array is expected to offset 67% of the power used by the school annually. In its first year, the school’s solar system is projected to produce 421 MWh of clean energy, enough to power 44 average Virginia homes.

Under the PPA, the solar power equipment will be provided at no upfront cost to Roanoke Schools. For the 25-year term of the agreement, Pardee Renewable Energy and Secure Solar Futures will own, operate and maintain the solar power system and will sell the energy produced to the school division at a rate lower than the local electric utility.

Preston Park Elementary School is expected to save $581,000 electricity costs over the term of the PPA. After the conclusion of the term, Roanoke Schools can opt to take ownership of the solar power system at a nominal cost, producing its own energy for the remainder of the equipment’s lifespan.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.
