#5637. 7 Of The Rarest Fox Body Mustangs Ever Built And What They're Worth Today

The Ford Mustang has enjoyed a long career as America's pony car since it debuted in 1964. It lived through the Malaise era, took on competition from all manner of American rivals, and even went on to find considerable success in other markets – no easy feat for a muscle car. Through those years, we've been blessed with a total of seven Mustang generations, but we shouldn't pretend each one was as brilliant as the next. While the first-gen model has gone down as an important slice of American motoring history, the Mustang II that followed is largely best left forgotten. However, the third generation, known as the Fox Body Mustang, rescued the Mustang's reputation as a muscle car and stood the test of time.

These cars debuted as '79 model years, and were not replaced until the SN95 cars marched onto the scene for 1994. Throughout those years, an estimated 2.6 million Fox Body Mustangs found owners, making it by far one of the most successful Mustang generations. Among them, though, were some exceedingly rare variants, such as the immensely sought-after 1-of-107 SVT Cobra R, and the 3,003 Feature Cars of '93, and the 1985 Twister II Mustang.

These Mustangs, along with quite a few others that are rarer than many multi-million-dollar super- and hypercars, stand as the rarest Fox Body cars ever produced. Starting with the more common and leaving the rarest for last, here are seven Fox Body Mustang variants with seriously scant production numbers, and how much they're worth today.

If we were to split the Turbo GT up by model year, the '83 Mustang Turbo GT would sit much farther up this list, since only 556 examples made it out of the factory gates. However, with 3,972 examples produced the following year — split between 3,241 hatchbacks and 731 convertibles – the total Turbo GT production comes out to 4,528 examples.

As the name suggests, instead of relying on old-school six- or eight-cylinder lumps that traditionally featured in Mustangs, the Turbo GT took advantage of boost. Under its hood sat a fuel-injected and turbocharged 2.3-liter inline-4, and when fed the correct premium gas, it was good for 145 horsepower and 180 pound-feet of torque. It was actually quite a technically impressive mill for the time, sporting forged aluminum pistons, a lightweight flywheel, valves with a higher heat resistance, and extra oil cooling hardware — this was certainly no half-measure attempt from the Blue Oval engineers.

Ford sadly canned the Turbo GT after the '84 model year. While a smaller four-cylinder engine was no doubt more economical than a naturally aspirated V8, and yet still punchy enough to induce a smile on the face of most drivers, less than 5,000 sales across both model years clearly meant it wasn't a great car to market to the mass market. Such scarcity has not translated into high desirability in the modern day, either, since any recent sales we found on Bring a Trailer barely scraped past $12,000.

The 1992 Ford Mustang Summer Edition, or, to call it by its full name, the 1992 Vibrant Red Mustang LX 5.0 Limited Edition Convertible, was exactly that — a vibrant red drop-top 'Stang. It was powered by a 225-hp, 300-lb-ft, 302-cubic-inch High Output (H.O.) V8, and it debuted halfway through the 1992 model year during the — you guessed it — summer.

Other unique features include a white leather interior with contrasting black stitch-work and a complementary white fabric roof. It was by no means subtle, especially with 16-inch Opal Pearlescent white wheels completing the bold look. Also helping the Summer Edition Mustang differentiate itself from similarly specified but otherwise completely regular Mustangs was a unique decklid spoiler, body-colored mirrors, plus a variety of other Vibrant Red exterior trims too.

Ford churned out just 2,193 of these striking convertibles, and of those, the manual-equipped cars are by far the rarest. Buyers had the choice of a four-speed automatic or five-speed manual, and only 787 of them chose the three-pedal version. Today, the Summer Edition is quite a desirable little classic, and although it doesn't exactly command huge premiums, the finest examples are starting to require larger investments when they do change hands. For example, recent online auction results courtesy of Bring a Trailer reveal that smart, low-mileage cars sell for in excess of $20,000 fairly regularly, while the very best of the bunch can sometimes see the hammer fall at double this price.

Much like the aforementioned 1992 Summer Edition, Ford offered a pair of other such special editions in the following year. These colorful special editions are sometimes called "Feature Cars," and for '93, Vibrant Red stepped aside for Vibrant White and Canary Yellow. Their features were really quite similar to the '92 car, with a 205-hp 5.0-liter V8 providing propulsion, and a smattering of white visual details. Once again, they were all LX-based convertible cars.

In total, Ford produced 3,003 of these 1993 Feature Cars, which, of course, makes them less rare than the aforementioned '92 Vibrant Red model. However, if we break production down by color, we find 1,503 Canary Yellow examples, and 1,500 in Vibrant White, so the individual models are actually rarer than the special edition of the previous year. We also find that, again, those equipped with the five-speed manual are rarest, with an estimated 565 yellow manuals, of which only 113 sported the white interior and top combination. As for the Vibrant White car, just 404 were picked up with the stick.

While collector examples with minimal mileage and low owner counts would surely match the prices paid for '92 cars, recent auction results reveal that most examples in either color seem to change hands for between $15,000 and $20,000. Such is the beauty of even the rarer guises of the Fox Body; it's a remarkably affordable yet fun and stylish classic, perfect for enthusiasts with realistic budgets.

The next Fox Body on our list takes us back into the 1980s, and behind the wheel of another performance-focused model. It's the 1985.5 Mustang SVO, the ".5" referring to the fact Ford's SVO model was refreshed halfway through the model year. As for the SVO part, that stands for Special Vehicle Operations, a short-lived department which predated the far-better-known SVT department.

Under the '85.5 SVO's hood was once again fitted a turbocharged 2.3-liter inline-four, much like that of the aforementioned Turbo GT. However, in this application, the four-pot kicks out a much more meaningful 205 hp and 248 lb-ft of torque. Those specs are specific to the '85.5 car, and importantly, the output of these cars is higher than both the Mustang models that preceded and succeeded it.

Only 436 examples of the mid-year-refresh SVO Mustangs were ever produced, making them exceedingly rare classic Mustangs. Today, Hagerty tells us that average examples should still be trading south of $20,000, while the very best examples may command in excess of $60,000. For collectors, just finding one of these rare 1985.5 cars might prove tricky, as previous auction listings prove they don't come up for sale often.

The Mustang SVT Cobra R arrived in the Fox Body's final model year, right before the SN95 generation was ushered in for 1994. Ford also sold a non-R version dubbed the SVT Cobra, and while those cars were still high-performance street machines, the Cobra R was bred for the track. It still kept its street-legal status, but clearly outlining its purpose, the "R" stands for race-ready.

The SVT Cobra R was available exclusively in Vibrant Red, and it set buyers back the very significant sum of $25,692, which, for reference, was almost $10,000 more than a base GT Coupe. What buyers received for their cash, though, was a seriously potent performance package, which included a track-ready suspension with Eibach springs and Koni adjustable shocks and struts, vented brake rotors in all four corners, and uprated oil and power-steering cooling systems. When it comes to motivation, the SVT Cobra R shipped with a 5.0-liter V8 with 235 horsepower and 280 lb-ft of torque.

That was certainly more than the 205 horsepower and 275 lb-ft on offer from the '93 Mustang GT, but whether the hardware tweaks and bump in power were enough to justify the huge hike in price is another matter. Fortunately, Ford only had to convince 107 buyers to justify the expense, as that's how many SVT Cobra R Fox Bodies were built. Most went to collectors, and some found their way out onto the track. However, unlike many of the other rarities on this list, the SVT Cobra R commands significant sums whenever it crops up for sale. A low-mileage specimen achieved a $132,000 result at a 2019 Barrett-Jackson auction, which set something of a precedent. Hagerty now pegs concours examples at a steep $196,000, on average.

The original Twister Special Mustang arrived as a 1970 model year, made available through Kansas area dealers only. It might just be the rarest Mach 1 Mustang ever made, but 15 years later, Ford launched another Twister, which proved even rarer than the original. Once again, the Fox Body Twister II variant was only sold within the Kansas City sales district, and compared to the 96 examples of the 1970 Mach 1 Twister Special, Ford only produced 90 Fox Body Twister II models – a mix of three-door GT coupe hatchbacks and convertible models. However, unlike the 1970 Mach 1 Twister Special, which was based on the Mach 1, the Twister II was nothing more than a cosmetic package for a standard Mustang GT, with a special dash badge and Twister II-specific graphics on the exterior.

Breaking the production figures down further, Ford produced 76 coupes and 14 convertibles of the Twister II. Of those coupes, 74 were sold with a five-speed manual, whereas only nine convertibles were equipped with the stick. So, despite more ragtops being made, the rarest iteration is actually an automatic coupe, of which only two were ever sold.

Under the hood, all Twister II Mustangs sported the 5.0-liter H.O. Windsor V8, which was good for 210 hp when equipped with the manual 'box, or just 180 hp when an auto transmission was specified. Despite the model's incredible scarcity, Twister II Mustangs have been seen to regularly trade below $20,000 in fettle over the last few years.

By 1980, the Mustang II era was over, and Ford was keen to demonstrate that the Mustang name was still serious about performance. However, big-block V8s were no longer the flavor of the month, so instead, Ford set about developing a boosted inline-four-powered Mustang that would carefully tread the line between fuel efficiency and outright performance.

Teaming up with Michigan-based McLaren Engines allowed the Blue Oval brand to borrow from the company's motorsport expertise, while also attaching a performance-first name to the up-and-coming model, letting customers know the sedate Mustang was no more. The engine in question will sound familiar, since it's the same turbocharged 2.3-liter inline-four that was later revised for the Turbo GT and SVO. In the M81, it produced 175 horsepower, which was significant at the time, and the M81's visuals also screamed performance. Blistered arches partnered with hood scoops, wide BBS alloy wheels, and lowered suspension to ensure it gave the correct impression.

That said, a $25,000 price tag is what arguably destroyed the automaker's dreams of punching out 250 units. Just 10 were made, including one prototype. The M81 might well be the king of all Fox Bodies, but factoring in inflation makes for an MSRP north of $100,000, so perhaps it's no wonder the idea never took off. In 2024, one of these 10 Mustangs with just 561 miles from new sold for $82,500 courtesy of Mecum Auctions. In other words, even 44 years on, the pricey M81 hasn't proved to be much of a solid investment.

Eight of 10 patients with treatment-resistant/refractory Waldenström macroglobulinemia responded to an investigational CAR T-cell therapy. (Blood)

#5635. 9 Cheaper Alternatives To The Toyota Land Cruiser

The Toyota Land Cruiser is synonymous with toughness and reliability. It is a reputation it has been earning for more than 70 years. Toyota first used the Land Cruiser name in 1954 to describe the small all-wheel-drive truck developed for the Japan Ground Self-Defense Force. Over the decades, the Land Cruiser has grown more luxurious but never abandoned its rugged roots.

The current 250-series Land Cruiser sold in the U.S. is based on the smaller Prado model sold in other countries. It is not only physically smaller than the 200-series it replaced, but it also has a much lower price tag as well. In 2021, the 200-series Land Cruiser started at a little more than $87,000 with delivery, processing, and handling fees. Today, a new Land Cruiser 1958 starts at $59,775, all fees included. That is a significant price cut for what is still a very capable body-on-frame SUV.

The 2027 Land Cruiser is powered by a hybrid 2.4-liter turbocharged i-Force Max powertrain that produces a combined 326 horsepower and 465 pound-feet of torque. Meanwhile, standard full-time four-wheel drive with a two-speed transfer case and crawl control ensures it will get the entire family to the campsite — or the local Whole Foods.

For this list, we wanted to present buyers with a diverse set of alternatives that deliver many of the Land Cruiser's main strengths at a significantly lower price. These include models that prioritize rugged off-road ability, while others focus a bit more on practicality, comfort, and even a dose of luxury. There is something for everyone in this list, and every one of them will save you serious money. Without further ado, here are nine of those alternatives ranked from the most to the least expensive. All the MSRPs cited in this article include handling and destination fees.

We start the list by looking in Toyota's own backyard. Starting at $55,085, the Toyota 4Runner TRD Off-Road i-Force Max is the most expensive Land Cruiser alternative on our list, but it is still several thousand less than the base Land Cruiser. Although Toyota believes they appeal to different buyers, the 4Runner TRD Off-Road i-Force Max and the Land Cruiser share the exact same powertrain. The similarities continue with the same body-on-frame TNGA-F platform, two-speed transfer case, "crawl control," and more. However, the 4Runner actually holds the edge in ground clearance (9.1 inches versus 8.1 inches, respectively). In an off-road battle, there isn't going to be much in it between them.

The Land Cruiser has the edge in interior space, however. You get more head, shoulder, hip, and leg room compared to the 4Runner. The Land Cruiser also offers 46.2 cubic feet of cargo space behind the second row of seats compared to 42.6 for the 4Runner TRD Off-Road i-FORCE MAX. Although all 4Runner models do have that beloved power rear window.

For most buyers, the decision will likely come down to exterior styling and space requirements. The two rigs are very similar. If you need the extra space inside, the Land Cruiser is the smart pick. But if you can live with slightly tidier dimensions and need off-road capability, go with the 4Runner.

The 4Runner TRD Off-Road i-Force Max is as close as you can get to a Land Cruiser, but if you want even more dramatic savings, a 2026 4Runner SR5 with the $2,000 part-time four-wheel-drive option will serve you just fine. You lose the i-Force Max engine, two-speed transfer case, and some other goodies. But you will only spend $45,865 and still get a viable Land Cruiser alternative.

Like the Land Cruiser itself, Jeep also got its start in the military, and today, the Jeep Wrangler Rubicon is one of the most serious off-road SUVs you can buy. A 2027 four-door model starts at $53,325. That is a $6,450 savings over the Land Cruiser. The Rubicon may not have the on-road refinement of the Land Cruiser, but thanks to a removable roof and doors, it has an even greater sense of adventure. Before you buy one, you might want to check if driving without doors is legal in your state.

A Wrangler Sport S is much less expensive than the Rubicon and is a capable vehicle in its own right. However, with front and rear electric-locking differentials, rock rails, skid plates, electronic front sway-bar disconnect, Dana 44 solid axles, and 33-inch all-terrain tires the Rubicon delivers the kind of off-road hardware that makes it a significantly more capable Land Cruiser alternative the trail gets serious. And while the 285-hp 3.6-liter V6 engine is down on power compared to the hybrid power plant in the Toyota, the Rubicon does come standard with a six-speed manual transmission for those that like to shift on their own.

If you like to bring your toys with you, it is important to note that the Land Cruiser can tow 6,000 pounds, but the Wrangler Rubicon can only manage 3,500. For the off-road enthusiast who wants the iconic Jeep looks, a manual transmission, and that open-air driving experience, the Wrangler Rubicon is a great choice. It is also less expensive than Toyota's rugged SUV, and you may even get some free rubber duckies along your travels.

For $51,590, you can get a 2027 Honda Passport TrailSport. It is the first vehicle on this list that doesn't use body-on-frame construction. It doesn't even have a dedicated four-wheel-drive system like every other SUV we have discussed so far. Sure, it has a torque-vectoring all-wheel-drive system, aggressive 31-inch tires, and 9.6 inches of ground clearance, but an AWD Honda isn't going to be any good off-road, right? Wrong.

The Passport TrailSport is plenty capable. It is $4,800 more expensive than the base Passport RTL. However, you get a significant amount of off-road upgrades for your money. All-terrain tires, off-road suspension, Honda's TrailWatch camera system, recovery hooks, skid plates, and more help justify the added expense for those interested in getting closer to matching the Land Cruiser's off-road prowess.

The 285 hp and 262 lb-ft of torque from the 3.5-liter V6 are on par with the Wrangler and noticeably less than what the Land Cruiser offers, but power isn't everything. The Passport TrailSport is capable of going further than where most owners are likely to ever go. The Honda even has a respectable 5,000-pound towing capacity.

The Land Cruiser can probably get you even further off-road than the Passport TrailSport, but for over $6,000 you have to ask yourself how often you will take advantage of that extra capability. If you don't really need that low-range transfer case and more sophisticated off-road hardware, the Honda Passport TrailSport might be the smarter choice for your family. And if off-roading is not on the menu at all, the $46,790 2027 Honda Passport RTL can save you even more money.

The Jeep Grand Cherokee Limited can handle some trails, but unlike the Wrangler Rubicon, this is a Land Cruiser alternative for those who appreciate the Toyota's luxury and comfort over its rock-crawling abilities. The Grand Cherokee has been an icon ever since it drove through a glass window for its premier at the 1992 Detroit Auto Show. Today, it continues to be respected for its mix of on-road manners and off-road ability.

A two-row 4x4 Limited starts at just $48,315. That is more than $10,000 less than the Land Cruiser. The Jeep is powered by the slightly unrefined new turbocharged 2.0-liter Hurricane four-cylinder. It makes good power, producing 324 hp and 332 lb-ft of torque. Despite a torque deficit of over 130 lb-ft, the Grand Cherokee Limited can tow up to 6,200 pounds– 200 more than the Land Cruiser.

Interior dimensions between the two SUVs are close. The Jeep has an advantage in some areas, such as rear legroom, and the Toyota has an advantage in overall interior space and cargo volume. But the biggest difference between these machines is in the off-road equipment. The unibody Jeep doesn't have a two-speed transfer case or locking differentials. The Jeep will take you off-road, but when the trail gets really tough, you might want the Toyota. Or take your $10,000 savings and buy a Can-Am Outlander Pro Quad and go just about anywhere you want.

When the Nissan Pathfinder first came out 40 years ago, it was a rugged body-on-frame SUV based on the Hardbody pickup truck. However, for the last 30 years, the Pathfinder has been a unibody model with a priority on ride comfort over off-road use. Then, in 2019, Nissan released the Rock Creek option package to give the Pathfinder a bit more off-road mojo. In 2023, the Rock Creek become a separate trim level.

A 2026 Pathfinder Rock Creek will set you back $46,665. The Rock Creek trim sits 0.63 inches higher off the ground than other Pathfinder models. It also comes with wider all-terrain tires and hill descent control. The 3.5-liter six-cylinder engine is tuned for a bit more power, too, producing 295 hp and 270 lb-ft of torque compared to 284 hp and 259 lb-ft in other Pathfinders.

If you don't need these off-road upgrades, you might want to look at the base Pathfinder SV. It still comes with four-wheel drive, but it starts at $3,100 less than the Rock Creek. The savings continue at the gas pump, as the SV gets 26 mpg combined versus 23 mpg for the Rock Creek. The SV also runs on regular gasoline, not the premium grade the Rock Creek requires.

Probably the most significant differentiator in daily driving between the Nissan and the Toyota is the fact that the Nissan is a three-row SUV. If you need that third row, then a Land Cruiser just isn't going to work for you. With its third-row flexibility, reasonable off-road abilities, and more than $13,000 in your bank account, the Pathfinder represents an attractive trade-off.

The value leader in the Subaru Outback range is the base model that starts at just $34,490. For that money, you get a 2.5-liter boxer-4 engine making 180 hp and 178 lb-ft of torque. It comes with Subaru's famous symmetrical all-wheel drive with X-Mode and hill descent control. But if you want a rig that competes a bit better in the off-road and luxury departments, you will want to step up a few trim levels.

At $45,490, the Outback Wilderness is still 25% cheaper than the Land Cruiser, but it will do 90% of the work. We are not financial advisors, but that sounds like a deal to us. The Wilderness features a 260-hp turbocharged 2.4-liter boxer-4, a four-wheel independent raised suspension with electronically controlled dampers, and a 3,500-pound tow rating — 800 pounds more than the base car. The Wilderness trim also offers 9.5 inches of ground clearance. That is over an inch more than the standard Land Cruiser (8.3 inches).

Of course, ground clearance alone doesn't make a great off-roader — a giraffe has plenty of ground clearance, but we wouldn't want to ride one on the Rubicon Trail. Although all Subaru Outbacks are unibody crossovers and feature the brand's famous symmetrical AWD, the Wilderness has underbody protection, all-terrain tires, and high-clearance front and rear bumpers to give it some extra off-road chops over the base Outback.

If you want to have a more luxurious experience, you can add the optional $2,090 OP22 or $4,090 OP23 package to the Wilderness. The former will load it up with a 12.1-inch touch-screen display with 360-degree surround view of the car and a power moonroof. The latter will also add Nappa leather, ventilated front seats, and power-folding exterior mirrors into the mix. And it will still be nearly $10K less than the Toyota. It won't conquer the same extreme terrain as the Land Cruiser, but most buyers would rather have the 10 grand.

A four-door Ford Bronco has a starting price of just $43,290. Like the Jeep Wrangler Rubicon, the Bronco's party trick is its removable top and doors. It is tough to beat that open-air feeling. Although having an extra $16,485 in your pocket is darn close. The Bronco is yet another body-on-frame SUV, but it has a rougher and more tumble demeanor than the Land Cruiser.

The base Bronco is plenty capable and will serve the off-road needs of most people. The standard 300-hp and 325-lb-ft 2.3-liter EcoBoost turbocharged inline-4 provides plenty of thrust, and it comes mated to a seven-speed manual transmission, while a 10-speed automatic is a $1,795 option. For even more off-road capability, stepping up to the $50,885 Badlands trim will give you electronic locking differentials, a disconnecting front stabilizer bar, and Bilstein position-sensitive dampers.

One area where the Bronco falls short is in towing capacity. The Ford SUV is rated to tow just 3,500 pounds, giving the Land Cruiser a 2,500-pound advantage. But if you don't need that extra towing capability, the Bronco is a viable alternative to the Land Cruiser. Your biggest issue will be deciding how to spend all that money you saved.

And now for something completely different: a pickup truck. At first glance, a Chevrolet Colorado WT 4WD may not seem like a logical alternative to the Toyota Land Cruiser. But once you dig into the details and factor in the value proposition, it starts to make a lot more sense. The Colorado WT 4WD can be had for just $36,795 a whopping $21,980 less than the Land Cruiser. It's a pickup, but it comes in a four-door configuration just like the Land Cruiser. It also uses body-on-frame construction  and boasts a slightly lower 7.9 inches of ground clearance. Add the $945 Advanced Trailering Package, however, and the truck will tow up to 7,700 pounds. Of course, the main benefit of picking a Colorado over the Land Cruiser is a five-foot cargo bed, which gives it a significant utility advantage for buyers who need their family vehicle to also perform work duties.

The turbocharged 2.7-liter TurboMax inline-4 makes 310 hp and 430 lb-ft of torque. That's just 16 hp and 35 lb-ft shy of the Land Cruiser's hybrid powertrain. There is seating for five, and while the overall length of the truck is about two feet longer than the Land Cruiser, the interior dimensions are very close. The trade-off is that the Colorado doesn't have the enclosed storage space the Land Cruiser has. Although for $1,295, you can add a hard folding lockable tonneau cover to the truck bed, making that a non-issue.

If you are willing to think a bit outside the box, you can get yourself a suitable Land Cruiser alternative for a lot less money. And your partner won't freak out if you throw a pile of meat you find on the side of the road in the back of the pickup. Try that in the Land Cruiser.

That brings us to arguably the best value on our list. It is easy to dismiss the smaller Bronco Sport, but the Badlands trim with the Sasquatch Badlands Package can go further off-road than anyone really needs it to. And honestly, you don't even need that to get yourself a budget Land Cruiser alternative.

Yes, the Bronco Sport is a unibody SUV, but as we have seen with other vehicles on this list, that alone doesn't knock it out of contention. The Bronco Sport Big Bend will set you back just $33,690. You could darn near buy two of them for the price of the Land Cruiser. The Big Bend will also come with a HOSS off-road suspension and a proper 4x4 setup It might not have all the beef of its Bronco big brother, and if the roof and doors come off your Bronco Sport, you are in a world of trouble, but this SUV deserves serious consideration.

The turbocharged 1.5-liter EcoBoost inline-3 is good for 180 hp and 200 lb-ft of torque. That is well below that of the Land Cruiser. That is one reason why the Bronco Sport can only tow 2,200 pounds. Still, due to its boxy design, the Bronco Sport can offer more front and rear headroom than the Land Cruiser 1958, as long as owners don't opt for a moonroof in the Bronco Sport. Leg room is nearly identical front and back compared to the Toyota, too, but the Toyota does have a maximum cargo capacity advantage of at least 16.9 cubic feet.

So, if you need to do a lot of moderate towing and haul a lot of cargo, the Land Cruiser is going to better suit your needs. But if you don't need that, the Bronco Sport Big Bend is a compelling option for a whole lot less money.

#5634. The US Is Auctioning Seabed Near American Samoa. A $3 Million Bid Will Not Build a Mine.

On November 19, the US Marine Minerals Administration is scheduled to auction mineral rights covering about 31.5 million acres of seabed near American Samoa. The two areas have minimum bids of $3 million apiece, with 20-year lease terms. It is an impressive area and a modest opening price for a prospective new metals industry. The difference between the two numbers helps explain what the sale is actually offering.

The winning bidder will acquire a position from which to study mineral deposits and seek subsequent permissions. The government has said explicitly that an issued lease does not authorize commercial harvesting. It allows preliminary work such as mapping, while physical exploration, testing and mining require additional plans and approvals. The lease does not establish reserves, a collector that can work for years, a processing plant or buyers willing to pay enough for the metals.

The announcement is being treated as an American critical-minerals milestone. Investors should pay close attention to the difference between a government auction and an operating mining project. A mineral lease can have speculative value long before a profitable business exists.

In my new TFIE Strategy Briefing on the American Samoa auction, I examine how federal leasing can advance while the integrated business case remains weak.

The acreage has no direct relationship to the amount of commercially recoverable metal. The final federal notice describes northern and southern areas of roughly 16.3 million and 15.2 million acres. They are regulatory boundaries. A mining company needs deposit-grade and abundance data, continuity, recoverable tonnage, processing recoveries and a market price that leaves a margin after all the offshore and downstream costs.

The geology is becoming better understood. In April 2026, US Geological Survey scientists deployed 38 box cores in the Samoa Basin, of which 36 yielded enough material for nodule-density measurements. The expedition report is useful new evidence about the region. It is an expedition summary rather than a reserve statement for either lease area. Mineral distributions vary within the sampled region, and the better-studied Clarion-Clipperton Zone is not an adequate substitute for American Samoa’s own geological evidence.

Collection creates another set of costs. Impossible Metals proposes autonomous vehicles that select nodules and return to a support vessel. The Metals Company uses a more mature crawler-and-riser approach that has demonstrated recovery at pilot scale. Both must turn offshore demonstrations into dependable annual production, with maintenance, ship time, recovery systems and delays included. Their technologies should not be assigned the same engineering maturity, but neither has proved a profitable integrated mining-and-processing business.

In 2025, metallurgical engineer Lyle Trytten and I completed an independent techno-economic assessment commissioned by the National Ocean Protection Coalition. We examined both collection approaches, offshore operations, processing, metal markets and financial sensitivities. We judged commercial viability within the following 15 years highly unlikely. A new leasing process has not supplied the operating and financial evidence that was missing in that assessment.

The history of first-of-a-kind offshore production should influence the financing. Nautilus Minerals spent substantial capital on its Solwara-1 venture without reaching commercial production. Deep-sea mining depends on specialized machines, ships, handling systems, processing capacity and sales contracts working in sequence. The first production years of comparable projects are rarely smooth. The lease is one of the cheapest and earliest commitments in that chain.

American Samoa also has its own decision to make. Its government has supported scientific investigation while withholding support for commercial extraction at present. A lawsuit filed by Samoan chiefs and community groups contests the federal process. The resulting legal and territorial questions matter alongside engineering and economics. The environmental finding for preliminary leasing activities is not a finding about the effects of a future commercial mine.

Auction results will show what qualified bidders will pay for an opportunity. A winning bid can justify a company’s further studies and improve its fundraising story. An operating business will require years of capital investment, production evidence and profitable metal sales. Those are much harder milestones, and they remain ahead of the bidders.

Read A Seabed Lease Does Not Fix a Broken Business Case for the full assessment of the auction, and the Barnard–Trytten report for the underlying engineering and financial analysis.

#5633. Democrat Pitches 'TrumpCare': Universal Healthcare From Cradle to Grave

— Newly elected members of Congress are looking for ways to collaborate with the president

Abdul El-Sayed, MD, DPhil, has proposed "TrumpCare," a golden insurance card providing universal healthcare, dangling the president's name on the ambitious Democratic plan.

James Talarico has suggested Donald Trump could become a man of history like Lyndon B. Johnson, who was seen as a master of congressional deal-making.

And even Hakeem Jeffries, in line to become the House speaker in January, has left the door open to working with the other side of the aisle "wherever and whenever possible" to deliver for the American people.

Democrats who swept into power by riding voter backlash to Trump are now pitching ways to collaborate with the president, playing to his vanity and his love of making deals to propel their own agenda.

The offers coming from the Democrats carry both practical and political applications -- a strategy to show that the party is serious about the hard work of legislating and also an acknowledgment that Trump's signature is needed for new laws.

Trump himself has said he doesn't want to spend the final 2 years of his presidency simply vetoing bills from the Democrats in control of Congress. It was only a few short years ago, during his first term, that Trump stunned Republicans by cutting deals with the Democratic leaders he called "Chuck and Nancy" -- Senate Leader Chuck Schumer and then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi.

Before the next Congress begins in January, Democrats are trying to entice Trump with the prospect of timeless glory to a president fixated on making his mark on Washington.

All he has to do, they say, is work with them to deliver sweeping policies like Medicare for All.

"Donald, this is on the table. Guarantee healthcare for every American, from cradle to grave," El-Sayed, who won the U.S. Senate race in Michigan after Trump branded him a terrorist sympathizer, wrote on social media.

He attached mock-ups of a gold-colored card, with Trump's name and signature far more prominent than the small text reading "Medicare for All."

Universal healthcare has long been anathema to Republicans, although Congress -- and even the Republican president himself -- will face pressure to deal with rising costs.

About 6 in 10 voters nationally said the federal government should play a bigger role in helping Americans get healthcare than it does now, according to the AP Voter Poll, a survey of more than 100,000 midterm voters.

The costs of expanding Medicare to cover more people would depend on the scope, and Democrats have considered plans that include dropping the eligibility age to below 65 or including children and young people. The Congressional Budget Office estimated in 2022 that various so-called single-payer options could increase federal subsidies for healthcare from $1.5 trillion to $3 trillion by 2030, while the total out-of-pocket costs for patients would decline.

Trump's willingness to slog through extended negotiations with Democrats is an open question, and his past promises of deal-making have been hit and miss. He reiterated Thursday he is willing to work with Democrats.

"I'm going to be vetoing a lot of things that they're going to send me initially that are bad for the country, and I think eventually they're going to call in, they're going to say, 'Let's make a deal,' and we'll be able to make deals on healthcare, on drug prices," the president told commentator Stephen A. Smith in an interview.

Trump's choice of Stephen Miller as his new White House chief of staff may foreclose some of those opportunities, however. Jeffries described Miller as a "stone-cold racist" and said he has "zero interest" in working with him.

The pitches from some of Democrats' most high-profile new arrivals in Washington will help shape the party's approach for the rest of Trump's presidency.

Talarico, the Democrat elected to the U.S. Senate from Texas, described the ways that Johnson is memorialized on Capitol Hill and remembered for shaping civil rights and social services as a senator and president.

"If President Trump wants the same plaques and portraits and busts as LBJ, we can help make that a reality," Talarico said Thursday at the What's Next conference in Denver, where many Democrats gathered to plot their way forward.

Talarico made his pitch to Democrats as much as to Trump. After getting the fulsome repudiation of Trump they've been seeking for a decade, the party's base voters may not be eager to see their newly elected leaders standing with him for a bipartisan signing ceremony at the White House Rose Garden that he paved over to create a patio resembling his Mar-a-Lago resort.

However, Talarico said Americans are counting on bold changes before the next president takes office after Trump's second term ends.

"Some of these reforms may not pass until 2029, but the American people cannot wait 2 years for change," he said. "So we must work with President Trump."

The eagerness to work with Trump is notable from El-Sayed and Talarico, who were among the president's most frequent targets as he hop-scotched the country for Republicans ahead of the midterms. He singled them out for especially personal attacks, questioning El-Sayed's loyalty to the U.S. and labeling the Texan as "Talafreako."

#5632. Zoox Launches Robotaxis in Atlanta

The year of the robotaxi rolls on. Just after writing about Zoox expanding its robotaxi service in Las Vegas, I see news that Zoox is entering Atlanta!

“Our initial rollout will focus on the Northwest region of Atlanta, near Atlantic Station, Woodward, and Home Park. We will expand throughout the year as our testing progresses,” Zoox shares.

To start with, in order to work out any bugs and avoid controversy, the service can only be used by Zoox employees, family members, and friends. But that should change fairly soon. “We look forward to opening our waitlist for Atlanta in the coming months, ahead of the launch of our Zoox Explorers early rider program.”

I do find it interesting that Zoox, Waymo, and Tesla are all in a sort of fast (but sort of small) expansion phase at the same time. They each keep adding more cities, but with quite limited geographic ranges and vehicle fleets. Is it all the early budding of robotaxi services that will bloom into giant networks? Or is it companies rushing to try to prove their viability and build up economies of scale, but which might not actually be financially viable and profitable in the long run — or even next year? We’ll have to wait to see. In the meantime, it’s certainly exciting each time a new area is opened up and more electric robotaxis hit the road.

In case you have forgotten where Zoox is in operation, the company informs us: “Atlanta marks our newest testing market for the robotaxi. This is in addition to testing in Austin and Miami, our public service in Las Vegas, and our Zoox Explorers early rider program in San Francisco.”

Also of note is the fact that Zoox didn’t just decide last month to go to Atlanta and rush into the market. It has been laying the groundwork for this launch for about a year and a half in the city. “We started operations in Atlanta in the Spring of 2025, using our testing fleet to map and learn about local driving behaviors, weather patterns, and the city infrastructure. Since then, we have been steadily expanding our driving capabilities, including beginning autonomous driving, and growing our geofence, fleet size, and team to prepare for this next stage of testing.” Zoox explains further how this is central to how Zoox operates:

“Zoox has always been thoughtful and measured in our approach to rolling out autonomous technology at every level. Before we even hit the road and begin driving with our testing fleet in any capacity, we first engage with local and state officials and regulators. Our philosophy is ‘no surprises’ as we lay the foundations for future service markets.

“Along with engaging with local and state officials and regulators, we also love partnering with the community. Workforce development starts long before a first job. That’s why we’re partnering with @Promise Center to help local youth learn, grow, and prepare for their futures in a safe, supportive environment. This partnership reflects our commitment to the Atlanta community. We’re excited to continue investing in the next generation and the future of this city.”

Circling back to Atlanta and why the company has made that one of the first cities for its robotaxi deployment, Zoox adds the following: “Atlanta is a dynamic, vibrant city. Known as the ‘Silicon Valley of the South,’ it boasts one of the largest ride-hailing markets in the United States. Atlanta’s dense, urban layout creates strong demand for efficient transportation solutions, which is where on-demand autonomous ride-hailing comes in. It also has a growing population, and demand like this is exactly why we’re excited to bring Zoox to Atlantans.”

Lastly, as I also highlighted in my article last night about Zoox expanding in Las Vegas, one key thing Zoox is proud of and argues sets it apart from the competition is how vertically integrated its robotaxi business is and how useful the design of its vehicle is for its purpose. Here’s more: “While Atlanta has become a hub for autonomous testing, Zoox continues to stand apart from other companies. Unlike the retrofitted solutions currently on the road, our robotaxi was built from the ground up for fully autonomous ride-hailing. It has no steering wheel or driver’s seat, instead featuring spacious, carriage-style seating for up to four passengers. This design layout allows us to offer Zoox passengers more than just a ride; we are providing a private space where riders can socialize, relax, and reclaim their time. […]

“Zoox owns, operates, and assembles its vehicles, and is the only company currently deploying a fully driverless, purpose-built robotaxi on public roads in the U.S.

“We see promise in bringing our robotaxi service to Atlanta, and look forward to offering Atlantans this unique and private space to socialize, rock out, and create their own experience while being transported to their destination.”

You can learn more on the Zoox website or the Zoox app.

#5631. SpaceX's Rivals Are Already Filling The Void Left By Falcon 9

The future of American access to space is in doubt for the first time in decades. That very uncertainty is already starting to show itself as an opportunity for the companies positioned to seize it. In the last few weeks, the Pentagon has handed out two separate $150 million loans to rocket companies Stoke Space and Rocket Lab, investments meant to build out their launch infrastructure and manufacturing capabilities. That ought to help get them to a place where they can reliably launch military assets into space, but critically, it also helps them attract and deliver on commercial contracts, too. Business is booming, and a lot more is expected to come the way of these two and many other rocket companies in the next few years. The main reason: SpaceX, the dominant force in the entire sector, is giving that business away.

Elon Musk's rocket enterprise has expanded a lot lately, getting into everything from AI infrastructure to natural gas mining. Its future mainly depends on the in-development Starship rocket, however. The largest and most powerful rocket ever built, it is supposedly taking astronauts to the Moon and is the only rocket that can deploy Starlink's newest V3 satellites. SpaceX really, really needs Starship to work. So it's acting as if it will work, which means the company has already stopped taking reservations for flights on its workhorse Falcon 9 rocket. While it appears that the military and NASA may get a few extra years to use that rocket, Musk has made it clear he intends to wind down the Falcon 9 sooner rather than later.

The question is, what happens if Starship isn't ready in the next few years? Even if it is, what if it's so busy launching Starlink satellites that it won't take other customers? Plus, as a larger rocket, Starship will likely be more expensive to commission than other rockets. Where does the rest of the space ecosystem, including the military, go once the Falcon 9 is gone?

The Pentagon sees the coming crunch in launch capacity and is moving now to mitigate it. It wants more companies capable of delivering its payloads to orbit to reduce its dependence on one provider and, really, one particular business leader. That's why its Office of Strategic Capital, the military's investment arm established in 2022, is pouring resources into SpaceX's smaller rivals right now. For Stoke Space, this money is intended to help it build out a new launchpad at the Space Force base in Cape Canaveral, where its in-development Nova Block 2 rocket will operate. For Rocket Lab, the money will help it upgrade its factories and expand its drone boat fleet for the recovery of returning rockets.

It's not a coincidence that Stoke's Nova Block 2 and Rocket Lab's in-development Neutron rocket are more or less competitors to the departing Falcon 9, not Starship. Though they are a bit smaller, capable of 15 and 13 metric-ton payloads respectively compared to the Falcon 9's 23, that still puts them in position to scoop up business for medium-sized missions. For a lot of satellite and other space companies, that's all they need, so it's a better fit for them than a more expensive Starship launch. And that's assuming Starship would even take reservations for launches beyond its Starlink needs.

Still, that word "in-development" keeps cropping up, which is its own cause for concern. All of these rockets need to actually prove they can launch and recover reliably, or there's no business at all. The Neutron rocket already delayed its debut to 2027; the Nova Block 2 isn't scheduled to start flying until 2029. It really puts into perspective how far ahead of its time the Falcon 9 was. Of course, there are other competitors knocking at the door, most notably Jeff Bezos' Blue Origin and its New Glenn rocket. What will determine the next decade of the space industry is how many of these rockets come online when and how successfully.

As the Russo-Ukrainian War has proven, space is a critical component of modern warfare; in multiple instances, Ukraine's sudden disconnection from Starlink (reportedly at Musk's behest) directly impacted its ability to conduct strike operations. Those instances also prove the fragility of depending on one company. So these investments are meant to diversify the Pentagon's corporate partners in this domain, giving it several ways to reach and, in the future, utilize space. Redundancy is going to be key to military space endeavors in the event of a war with a near-peer adversary.

However, it's worth noting that investments in launch infrastructure and manufacturing also set up these companies for success in their launches for commercial clients, which in turn nurtures the whole space industry. As a primary strategic concern for the 21st century, the American government is essentially subsidizing the growth of the entire space sector. The more companies are active here, the more innovations and technologies are created. Those then become the engines of both economic and military might. As ever with the military-industrial complex, it feeds on itself.

And with the Falcon 9 fading away, the feeding is good. Rocket Lab's Neutron rocket, which hasn't even flown yet, is already completely booked through the rest of this decade. It also increased its launch fee from $55 million to $70 million, because it knows that Falcon's absence gives it pricing power. If you're a SpaceX competitor who's felt crushed by the dominance of the SpaceX workhorse for all these years, the boom times are coming. The hard part, of course, will be meeting all that new demand. The Falcon 9 launched 165 times last year. The booked-out Neutron is on the docket for... 16 launches over the next few years.

#5630. Study Pinpoints States with Steepest Warming Trends Since 1980

The findings indicate where adaptive measures are needed most around the United States.

Global warming, the observed increase in the Earth’s average global surface temperature since preindustrial (1850-1900) times, currently stands at around 1.63 degrees Celsius. While this number is expected to continue to rise, local temperatures will not go up uniformly around the world. In regions where warming trends are most pronounced, local adaptation and resilience measures will be especially critical to minimizing risks to ecosystems, agricultural productivity, the built environment, human health, and economic well-being.

To that end, a new open-access study in the journal Scientific Data identifies the highest-risk regions for warming within the United States. Using data from well-known, high-resolution climate and weather datasets (MERRA2 and ERA5) covering the years 1980-2022, researchers at the MIT Center for Sustainability Science and Strategy (CS3) report global warming-attributed trends in monthly-averaged maximum daily temperature for every U.S. state.

The study finds that Alaska, Utah, New Mexico, Colorado, and Nevada have experienced the strongest average warming trends; the lowest occurred in Hawaii, Montana, North Dakota, South Dakota, and Arkansas. Across the country, warming trends were most pronounced during the summer and fall, particularly in September and November. Alaska has the highest historical maximum daily temperature warming (an average of 2.5 C of monthly-averaged maximum daily temperature increase per degree of global warming), while Hawaii has the lowest average historical warming, at 0.8 C.

The authors expect that barring unforeseen local anomalies, these state-by-state temperature trends will continue unabated as ongoing global emissions from human activities continue to raise the average global temperature.

“Based on these results, we encourage decision-makers to assess states that have undergone the steepest increases in maximum and minimum daily temperatures, and identify where continued trends would indicate a rapid progression toward thresholds beyond which natural and managed resources, ecosystems, and infrastructure can function safely,” says MIT CS3 Deputy Director C. Adam Schlosser, a co-author of the study. “In such cases, triaging and adaptive measures would take a high priority. By directing resources to where they are needed most, state and regional planners could significantly reduce climate-related physical and financial risks.”

Unlike previous efforts to report temperature trends across the United States, this study aggregates average maximum, minimum, and mean daily temperature data at the state level for each month of the year, providing actionable information for planners in government, industry, and civil society.

By Mark Dwortzan | Center for Sustainability Science and Strategy, MIT

#5629. Shorter Cycle Maintains Efficacy of Colorectal Cancer Regimen

— 14-day cycle length for trifluridine-tipiracil plus bevacizumab also limited toxicity

A shorter cycle length for a standard treatment for refractory metastatic colorectal cancer was noninferior to the typical longer length for overall survival (OS), while limiting toxicity, the phase III PRABITAS trial from Japan showed.

Median OS was 10.3 months in patients who received the standard 28-day cycle length of trifluridine-tipiracil (FTD-TPI; Lonsurf) and bevacizumab versus 11.3 months for those who received a 14-day schedule (stratified HR 0.93, 95% CI 0.80-1.08), reported Hiroya Taniguchi, MD, of Aichi Cancer Center Hospital in Nagoya, and colleagues.

This met the prespecified noninferiority margin of below 1.25 for the upper limit of the confidence interval, they noted in The Lancet.

The 12-month OS rates were 44% in the 28-day group and 47.5% in the 14-day group.

Importantly, rates of grade ≥3 neutropenia and grade ≥2 anorexia were reduced in the shorter-course group.

"Taken together with previous evidence, these findings support adoption of the 14-day FTD-TPI plus bevacizumab schedule as the preferred standard of care for refractory metastatic colorectal cancer," Taniguchi and colleagues wrote.

FTD-TPI plus bevacizumab was established as the standard treatment for refractory metastatic colorectal cancer based on results from the phase III SUNLIGHT trial, in which patients treated with the regimen achieved a significant improvement in OS compared with FTD-TPI monotherapy. Data from a real-world analysis supported the SUNLIGHT results.

However, Taniguchi and colleagues noted that while the regimen -- which involves administering FTD-TPI twice daily on days 1-5 and 8-12, and bevacizumab on days 1 and 15 of a 28-day cycle -- was generally tolerable, it "carries a treatment burden that leaves room for further optimization."

In particular, it has been associated with increased hematologic toxicity, including grade ≥3 neutropenia.

"The clinical message stemming from PRABITAS is straightforward," wrote Paolo Ciracì, MD, and Chiara Cremolini, MD, PhD, of Azienda Ospedaliera Universitaria Pisana in Pisa, Italy, in an accompanying commentary. "Simplifying FTD-TPI administration in a 14-day schedule does not compromise treatment efficacy but does reduce the burden of adverse events and also the complexity of treatment delivery."

The study also reflects a move towards a more patient-centered approach by "shifting the focus from which therapies should be used, and in what sequence, to how effective therapies can be delivered to preserve patients' fitness and their ability to remain on treatment and receive subsequent lines," they added.

However, Ciracì and Cremolini noted that it remains an open question whether these results translate into a meaningful improvement in quality of life since Taniguchi and colleagues did not formally assess that endpoint.

Given that a 14-day schedule had been proposed to reduce toxicity, the researchers sought to determine whether the shorter course was noninferior to the conventional 28-day schedule for OS.

PRABITAS was conducted at 178 hospitals in Japan and included 972 patients assigned to receive FTD-TPI (35 mg/m2 orally twice daily) plus bevacizumab (5 mg/kg intravenously) on either a conventional 28-day schedule or a 14-day cycle schedule (FTD-TPI on days 1-5, and bevacizumab on day 1).

Median patient age was 68 years, the majority (57%) were men, and RAS mutations were present in 59% of patients. The median follow-up was 17.9 months.

Looking at secondary endpoints, median progression-free survival was 3.9 months in both groups (stratified HR 0.93, 95% CI 0.81-1.06). The median time to treatment failure was 3.6 months in the 28-day group and 3.8 months in the 14-day group (stratified HR 0.90, 95% CI 0.79-1.03).

Among 906 patients with measurable disease at baseline, the objective response rate was 2.4% in the 28-day group and 1.6% in the 14-day group, with no complete responses observed. The disease control rate was 46.6% and 52.1% in the two groups, respectively.

Grade ≥3 neutropenia occurred in 39% of patients in the 28-day group and 13% in the 14-day group, with grade 4 neutropenia occurring in 8% and 1%, respectively. Febrile neutropenia occurred in 5% of patients in the 28-day group versus 1% in the 14-day group.

Non-hematologic toxicities were also less frequent in the 14-day group.

Grade ≥2 anorexia occurred in 26% of patients in the 28-day group compared with 15% in the 14-day group, while nausea was reported in 18% and 12%, respectively, and diarrhea in 10% and 4%.

Two treatment-related deaths occurred in the 14-day group, including one gastrointestinal hemorrhage and one gastrointestinal perforation, both attributed to bevacizumab.

#5628. 1967 Ford Mustang Fastback For Sale With 44,000 Miles Shown Wraps A-Code 289 V8 In Glorious Red-On-Red

The word icon gets thrown around quite liberally these days, particularly in the automotive world, but if there's one car that's an undisputed icon, it's the original Ford Mustang. If you're reading Jalopnik, then odds are you're already well acquainted with the OG Mustang's lore as a symbol of American individuality and freedom that managed to earn 22,000 orders on the day of its debut at the 1964 World's Fair, but it's still notable. For anyone wanting to own their own piece of the enchanting original pony car, this single-family owned A-Code 1967 Ford Mustang Fastback that's listed for auction on Bring A Trailer is an exceedingly authentic and well-preserved example of the breed.

It's such a well-preserved Mustang that it was shown on the International Show Car Association (ISCA) circuit in the late '90s. It's powered by a factory A-code 289 cubic inch V8 that has done just under 44,000 miles in the nearly 60 years since it rolled off the line at Ford's production facility in May of 1967.

This 'Stang left that factory painted this shade of Vintage Burgundy, and the undercarriage photos reveal its virtually showroom-fresh condition. According to the listing, it was repainted in the same color in 1982, and as such, this is one seriously dashing car. Plus, in true '60s Americana fashion, its interior is finished in red, with "luxury" vinyl upholstery, burgundy carpets, as well as a red dashboard and door cards contrasted with chrome-and-satin metal details.

The only demerit against this one-owner car is its three-speed automatic transmission, but it ain't a big block, or even a K-code car. The 289 Challenger Special V8 breathes through a four-barrel carburetor that was replaced in 2024, according to the ad, when the fuel tank, fuel lines, and fuel pump were all replaced, too. This is more of a stylish cruiser than a drag strip dominator, and honestly, I think it's better for it. Whoever ends up winning this immaculate fastback will be the proud owner of a sparkling piece of American automotive history.

#5627. AI Businesses Still Bleeding Billions

AI is fun and cool when it creates useful things for you or answers tough questions quickly and well (let’s not focus on AI slop and false claims at the moment), but that doesn’t mean AI businesses are financially sustainable or sensible. AI companies are still heavily subsidizing their broad use, with the benefits for them being growing user bases and sky-high valuations. These companies are burning billions of dollars each quarter on the hype and promise that they will rule the world one day (quite literally).

OpenAI, the company behind ChatGPT, reported this week that it had $10.9 billion in operating losses last quarter. That’s up from $10.1 billion in operating losses in the second quarter.

One might think that means not a lot changed, and certainly nothing positive. However, the company’s revenue was actually up by 50%! So, even with a huge boost in revenue, gigantic losses grew! Furthermore, the company was apparently even using a tool of “last resort” here.

OpenAI started running ads in ChatGPT, something that the company was adamantly trying to avoid. CEO Sam Altman previously called the proposal to run ads “unsettling” and claimed that the company would only go that route as a matter of “last resort.” Well, it seems the company has reached that point of last resort. And that can’t be a good thing, right? Considering that OpenAI is still losing billions of dollars a quarter, it’s even more concerning. What comes after a “last resort” effort to turn finances around?

Frankly, I wish we could spend billions of dollars a quarter to make CleanTechnica into a behemoth of a media organization! That would be amazing — imagine all of the stuff we could do! But what are the chances we’d be losing billions of dollars a quarter in doing so? And how long could we operate like that before external sources of funding stopped covering the bill?

As we know, OpenAI is one of the “leaders” of the AI industry. If it is struggling like this, who isn’t, and what does that mean for the industry overall and where it’s headed?

The one big player that has been raking in the dough in the midst of this enormous AI craze is NVIDIA, and look at what’s now happening there! Where are things going to go with that company?

“The news comes not long after controversy over how OpenAI was projecting its earnings. In October, the company reported an annualized revenue, an estimate of how much money it will earn over a year, of about $50 billion — which was $20 billion short of what it told investors only a month before,” Futurism adds. “The discrepancy was taken as an admission that the business models of AI companies weren’t as robust as their leadership was making them out to be, fueling speculation of an impending bubble.”

Why can’t the leaders of these companies just ask their chatbots how to turn a profit and implement their answers? Well, one of the answers might have been regulatory capture and blocking others from entering the market, but that dream may now be out the window. In my humble opinion, 2027 could be a very interesting year for AI companies, and that might involve a big popping noise.

Zach is recognized globally as an electric vehicle, solar energy, and energy storage expert. He has presented about electric vehicles and renewable energy at conferences in India, the UAE, Ukraine, Poland, Germany, the Netherlands, the USA, Canada, and Curaçao.

#5625. Fabric Molds Could Bring Material-Saving Concrete Beams Within Reach

MIT engineers cast a concrete beam using a sewn fabric mold with one-third the amount of concrete and half as much steel as a conventional beam.

Topology optimization is a computer design method that can help engineers design beams using reduced amounts of concrete and steel. But those intricate shapes produced from the tool are often too complex or expensive to pour into conventional rigid molds. The few that have been built have mostly relied on specialized equipment, such as concrete 3D printers or machine-carved molds, equipment that is out of reach in regions where new construction is expected to grow.

Now, MIT engineers have shown that highly efficient beams can be cast in something more accessible: sewn fabric. In a paper published in the Journal of Building Engineering, the team designed and built a 3-meter (about 10-foot) test beam using fabric molds created through an automated pattern-making process. Compared with a conventional beam designed to carry the same weight, the optimized beam used about one-third as much concrete and half as much steel reinforcement. When tested, its measured response was within about 2 percent of what the team’s computer model predicted.

Fabric formwork, in which concrete is poured into a mold made of textile, has been explored for years to create beams that vary in depth, rather than in rectangular shapes common in construction. But because fabric deforms under the weight of wet concrete, that flexibility has made the final shape hard to predict.

“The key realization was that this flexibility is not just a limitation to manage; with the right computational pattern design, it can become a way to produce optimized structural forms,” says Josephine Carstensen, the Gilbert W. Winslow (1937) Career Development Professor in Civil Engineering and senior author of the study.

Fabric molds can also be inexpensive, lightweight, and fast compared to wood or steel molds. “You could sew a formwork together off site, pack it into a box, and bring it to a job site very easily,” says Jackson Jewett PhD ’25, co-author of the study. “We joke that we are developing the H&M of the construction industry.”

Cement production accounts for roughly 8 percent of human-caused carbon emissions.

“This work addresses concrete’s climate footprint primarily by reducing material demand,” Carstensen says. “Cement production is highly carbon-intensive, so using less concrete is an important part of decarbonizing construction.”

Where new construction happens matters, too. The world’s number of buildings is expected to nearly double by 2050, with about 85 percent of that growth in developing countries, where access to advanced construction equipment is often limited. That is why, Carstensen says, the team focused on an accessible, cost-conscious fabrication approach.

Concrete is strong when compressed, but weak when pulled, so it’s reinforced with steel that can handle the pulling forces. The researchers first mapped a path for the steel reinforcement using a method called graphic statics, which can generate structural designs almost instantly. They used it to shape the steel bar so that it carries an equal share of the weight along its entire length to keep the reinforcement simple enough for builders to make. Topology optimization, which works more slowly but can place material freely, was used to determine where concrete was needed and where material could be removed from areas where it contributes little to the structural performance.

“Both methods are good at different things, so we tried to combine the strengths of both into a single methodology,” says Jewett.

The resulting beam has a top section, narrow angled legs, and a thin bottom section for the steel reinforcement. The team’s automated process translated the 3D beam design into flat fabric pieces that can be cut and sewn into a mold, accounting for how the fabric will deform once concrete is poured in. A simple timber frame supported the finished fabric mold.

For Mark Hellrich SM ’26, the study’s lead author, the main hands-on challenge was getting the concrete mix right. Woven cotton fabric lets water seep through, so a mix that is too runny loses water through the mold. It also presses harder against the fabric, pushing it away from the intended shape.

The team used a moderately wet, sand-rich mix with only small stones, so the concrete could flow through the beam’s narrow, angled legs and thin bottom section without getting stuck. They then packed it in by hand, prodding each layer and tapping and kneading the fabric from the outside.

“This is where the flexibility of the mold becomes an advantage: you can work the concrete through the interconnected passages without a vibrator, which a rigid mold of this shape would need,” says Hellrich. A concrete vibrator is a tool typically used to shake air pockets out of freshly poured concrete in rigid molds.

Once the concrete was in place, the wet fabric revealed the beam’s final shape. “All three beams were free of wrinkles and creases,” says Hellrich. The top surface stayed close to the design, while the main difference was a downward sag along the bottom, with the angled legs folding slightly inward.

To find out how much sewing precision the beam needs, the team built three molds for the same design, made from 36, 56, and 76 fabric pieces. Comparing the 3D scans of the cast beams with the target design, the researchers found that more seams gave finer control over the details, but did not necessarily make the beam’s overall shape more accurate.

“More seams improve local control because they divide the fabric into smaller, more constrained panels. But a more complex pattern also has more pieces to align and more seam length, so small cutting, sewing, and assembly inaccuracies can accumulate across the mold, explains Carstensen.

The 56-piece mold demonstrated the best balance, capturing the important design geometry without the added time, effort, and potential errors associated with the most complex pattern.

The researchers then load-tested one of the beams, gradually stacking 36 water-filled buckets on top of it, up to about 30 percent of its design load to reflect everyday service conditions. Weighted lines hung from the beam’s underside to precise measuring gauges below, tracking how far the beam bent as the load was added and removed. At the beam’s center, the measured deflection — its downward movement under the load — was within about 2 percent of the computer model’s prediction.

The team also compared the cost of a single fabric-formed beam with a conventional one and found that location makes all the difference. In the Global South, where materials tend to cost more relative to labor, all three fabric versions were cheaper, with savings of about 10 to 20 percent. In the Global North, higher labor costs for sewing reversed that advantage, making the fabric versions about 16 to 55 percent more expensive.

The findings suggest that fabric formwork is most promising for custom-shaped concrete components produced as a one-off or in small batches, such as exposed beams, roof or long-span framing, pedestrian structures, and landscape elements. For the repetitive, rectangular beams found in most buildings, conventional reusable molds remain the more economical choice where labor is expensive.

“To get started, a builder would need the digital pattern for the target component, a sewing machine and suitable fabric, and a simple timber support frame,” Carstensen says. “That is a much lower equipment threshold than robotic fabrication, concrete 3D printing, or CNC-milled molds.”

The researchers describe the 3-meter beam as a proof of concept comparable to a short building span. It is also a big step up from the 1.2-meter beam the team built in a previous project, Jewett notes.

Building bigger isn’t just a matter of enlarging the design, Carstensen notes. As a beam gets longer, its own weight and the pressure on the fabric mold change, so a larger beam would need a new design. The next step, Carstensen says, is “to test multiple beams to failure.”

“The nice thing about these optimization methods is they generate structures that look organic,” Jewett says. “Their geometries are clear expressions of how the forces move through the structural system. There is elegance to them. It makes you ask why we don’t design all our structures in this way.”

Joining Carstensen, Jewett, and Hellrich on the paper are co-authors Abhinit Sati, Edward Chien, and Emily Whiting, of Boston University’s Department of Computer Science.

The work was partially supported by the U.S. National Science Foundation.

By Stephanie Martinovich | Department of Civil and Environmental Engineering, MIT
